\mathcal{E}(\delta,n^{-1};\bm{\lambda})=\#{\left\{j\in N:\lambda_{j}\geq\delta\right\}}n^{-1}+\sum_{j\in N}(f_{j}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\lambda_{j}<\delta\right\}}

For the first term, using
\lambda_{j}\asymp j^{-\gamma}
, we have

\#{\left\{j\in N:\lambda_{j}\geq\delta\right\}}n^{-1}\asymp\delta^{-1/\gamma}n^{-1}.

For the second term, we use
f_{j(\ell)}^{*}\asymp\ell^{-(p+1)/2}
,
j(\ell)\asymp\ell^{q}
to find

\sum_{j\in N}(f_{j}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\lambda_{j}<\delta\right\}}\asymp\delta^{p/(q\gamma)}.

Balancing the two terms, we find that

\delta^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda})\asymp n^{-\frac{q\gamma}{p+q}},\qquad\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda})\asymp n^{-\frac{p}{p+q}}.

Now, since
\lambda_{j}(t)
is monotonically increasing, we have

\mathcal{E}(\delta,n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t))\geq\#{\left\{j\in N:\theta_{j}(t)^{2}\geq\delta\right\}}n^{-1}\geq\#{\left\{j\in N:\lambda_{j}\geq\delta\right\}}n^{-1}\geq C\delta^{-1/\gamma}n^{-1}.

Consequently, if
\mathcal{E}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t))\leq\mathcal{E}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(0))
, we have

\mathcal{E}^{*}(\delta,n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t))\geq cn^{-\frac{p}{p+q}}
and thus

\delta^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t))\geq Cn^{-\frac{q\gamma}{p+q}}.

Now, for a time interval
[0,T]
such that (
38
) holds
we will apply Lemma
A.3
and verify the conditions (1) and (2).
Let us take
L\asymp(n/\log n)^{\frac{q}{p+1}}
and let
N_{1}={\left\{j<L\right\}}
and
N_{2}={\left\{j\geq L\right\}}
.
For each
j\geq L
, we have

\absolutevalue{z_j}\leq\absolutevalue{f_j^*}+\absolutevalue{\ep_j}\lesssim L^{-\frac{p+1}{2q}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(jn)}{n}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(jn)}{n}}

Using Lemma
A.5
, we find that for
t\leq c\sqrt{n/(\log n)}
,

\lambda_{j}(t)=\theta_{j}(t)^{2}\leq\lambda_{j}\left[1+\exp(\sqrt{2} t \abs{z})\right]^{2}\leq C\lambda_{j}\exp(c \sqrt{\log j})<\delta_{0}=Cn^{-\frac{q\gamma}{p+q}},\quad j\geq L,

since
L\asymp(n/\log n)^{\frac{q}{p+1}}\gg n^{\frac{q}{p+q}}
by
q>1
.
Therefore, we have verified condition (2) in Lemma
A.3
.
On the other hand, since
L\lesssim n^{\frac{q}{p+1}}
, we have

\absolutevalue{\ep_j}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{C\log(jn)}{n}}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}},\quad j\leq L.

Now, for
j<L
such that
j\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{s}}(n^{-1})
, we have

\absolutevalue{f_j^*}\gtrsim L^{-\frac{p+1}{2q}}\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}.

Therefore, taking the constant factor in
L
small enough, we can find that

\absolutevalue{z_j}\geq\absolutevalue{f_j^*}-\absolutevalue{\ep_j}\geq\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{f_j^*}>C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}.

In the meantime, for
k<L
and
k\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{n}}(n^{-1})
, we have

\absolutevalue{z_k}=\absolutevalue{\ep_k}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}
.
Consequently, using Lemma
A.4
, we prove condition (1) in Lemma
A.3
.

Finally, let us show that the time interval
[0,T]
can actually cover
T=c\sqrt{n/(\log n)}
using a continuity argument.
Suppose that
\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t))
has a jump at time
t_{0}
, then, it can only increase by at most
\epsilon^{2}

(by the continuity of
\bm{\lambda}(t)
and that
\lambda_{j}(t)
,
\lambda_{j^{\prime}}(t)
do not coincide with probability one).
Therefore, we still have
\mathcal{E}^{*}(\delta,n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t))\lesssim n^{-\frac{p}{p+q}}
and that
\delta^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t))\geq Cn^{-\frac{q\gamma}{p+q}}
,
where the constant factor may increase.
Nevertheless, the second part of the argument still holds with the modified constant so that no up-crossing can happen at time
t_{0}
,
so
\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t))
can not increase at time
t_{0}
.

The feature error measure

We have already shown in the previous part that
\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda})\asymp n^{-\frac{p}{p+q}}
.
For
t=t_{*}\asymp\sqrt{n/(\log n)}
, let us take
\delta_{*}=Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.
We first consider
\mathcal{E}_{\text{V}}(\delta^{*},n^{-1};\bm{\lambda})
.
Ss discussed before, we have
\lambda_{j}(t_{*})<\delta_{*}
for
j\geq L
.
For
j<L
such that
\absolutevalue{f^*_j}\lesssim n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\log n)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we apply Lemma
A.5
to find

\lambda_{j}(t)=\theta_{j}(t)^{2}\leq 2\lambda_{j}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\#{\left\{j\in N:\theta_{j}(t_{*})^{2}\geq\delta_{*}\right\}}n^{-1}

\displaystyle=\#{\left\{j<L:\theta_{j}(t_{*})^{2}\geq\delta_{*}\right\}}n^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq n^{-1}\left[\#{\left\{j<L:\lambda_{j}\geq\delta_{*}/2\right\}}+\#{\left\{j<L:\absolutevalue{f^*_j}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\log n)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{*}^{-1/\gamma}n^{-1}+(n\log n)^{\frac{1}{p+1}}n^{-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-(1-1/(2\gamma))}+n^{-\frac{p}{p+1}}(\log n)^{-\frac{1}{p+1}},

Now, we consider
\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}(\delta^{*},n^{-1};\bm{\lambda})
.
For
j
such that
\absolutevalue{f^*_j}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\log n)^{3/2}
(which implies that
j<L
), we apply Lemma
A.6
to find

\theta_{j}(t)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{z_j}\geq\frac{1}{4}\absolutevalue{f_j^*}\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}.

Consequently,

\sum_{j\in N}(f_{j}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\lambda_{j}<\delta_{*}\right\}}\leq\sum_{j\in N}(f_{j}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\absolutevalue{f^*_j}\gtrsim n^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log n)^{3/2}\right\}}\lesssim n^{-\frac{p}{p+1}}(\log n)^{\frac{3}{2}\frac{p}{p+1}}\lesssim n^{-\frac{p}{p+1}}(\log n)^{\frac{2p}{p+1}}.

Therefore, we have

\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t_{*}))\leq\mathcal{E}(\delta_{*},n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t_{*}))\lesssim n^{-(1-1/(2\gamma))}+n^{-\frac{p}{p+1}}(\log n)^{\frac{2p}{p+1}}.

A.5
Deeper Over-parameterization under Sequence Model

In this subsection, let us consider deeper over-parameterization under sequence model.
We recall that the gradient flow dynamics are given by

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\beta}_{j}(t)&=-\nabla_{\beta_{j}}\mathcal{L}_{n}=\theta_{j}b_{j}^{D}(z_{j}-f_{j}),\quad\beta_{j}(0)=0;\\
\dot{\theta}_{j}(t)&=-\nabla_{\theta_{j}}\mathcal{L}_{n}=b_{j}^{D}\beta_{j}(z_{j}-f_{j}),\quad\theta_{j}(0)=\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}};\\
\dot{b}_{j}(t)&=-\nabla_{b_{j}}\mathcal{L}_{n}=D\theta_{j}b_{j}^{D-1}\beta_{j}(z_{j}-f_{j}),\quad b_{j}(0)=b_{0},\end{aligned}\right.

(39)

where
f_{j}=\theta_{j}b_{j}^{D}\beta_{j}
.
In this case, let us denote

\tilde{\bm{\lambda}}=(\tilde{\lambda}_{j})_{j\geq 1},\quad\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)=\left(\theta_{j}(t)b_{j}^{D}(t)\right)^{2},\quad\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(0)=\lambda_{j}b_{0}^{2D}

This gradient flow dynamics has been studied in the literature
[
34
,
33
]
,
and we will collect some results here.
First, we always have
\theta_{j}(t)\geq 0
and
b_{j}(t)\geq 0
, while the sign of
\beta_{j}(t)
is the same as that of
z_{j}
.
Moreover, the flow is symmetric in the sense that for the solution for
z_{j}<0
can be obtained by flipping the sign of
\beta_{j}(t)
.
Second, we can compute that

\frac{1}{2}\derivative{t}\theta_{j}^{2}=\frac{1}{2D}\derivative{t}b_{j}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\derivative{t}\beta_{j}^{2}=f_{j}(t)(z-f_{j}(t)),

showing that

\theta_{j}^{2}(t)-\beta_{j}^{2}(t)=\theta_{j}(0)^{2}-\beta_{j}(0)^{2}=\lambda_{j},\qquad b_{j}^{2}(t)-D\beta_{j}^{2}(t)=b_{j}(0)^{2}-D\beta_{j}(0)^{2}=b_{0}^{2}.

(40)

Now, let us introduce some lemmas.

Proposition A.7
.

Consider (
39
) and suppose that
b_{0}/\sqrt{D}\leq\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 1
,
we have

\tilde{\lambda}_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\theta_{j}(t)b_{j}^{D}(t)\leq C_{D}\max(\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{D},\absolutevalue{z}^{\frac{D+1}{D+2}},b_{0}^{-1}\absolutevalue{z}).

Proof.

Let us omit the subscript
j
for ease of notation.
Following the conservation quantity (
40
),

\min(\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}},\absolutevalue{\beta})\leq\theta\leq\sqrt{2}\max(\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}},\absolutevalue{\beta}),\quad\min(b_{0},\sqrt{D}\absolutevalue{\beta})\leq b\leq\sqrt{2}\max(b_{0},\sqrt{D}\absolutevalue{\beta}).

If
\beta\leq\min(\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}},b_{0}/\sqrt{D})
, we immediately have

\tilde{\lambda}=\theta b^{D}\leq 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{D}.

If
\beta\geq\max(\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}},b_{0}/\sqrt{D})
, we have

\absolutevalue{z}\geq\absolutevalue{f}\geq D^{\frac{D}{2}}\absolutevalue{\beta}^{D+2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\absolutevalue{\beta}\leq D^{-\frac{D}{2(D+2)}}\absolutevalue{z}^{\frac{1}{D+2}},

and thus

\tilde{\lambda}=\theta b^{D}\leq 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}D^{\frac{D}{2}}\absolutevalue{\beta}^{D+1}\leq 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}D^{\frac{D}{2(D+2)}}\absolutevalue{z}^{\frac{D+1}{D+2}}.

Otherwise, if
b_{0}/\sqrt{D}\leq\beta\leq\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we use

\absolutevalue{z}\geq\absolutevalue{f}\geq\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot(\sqrt{D}\absolutevalue{\beta})^{D}\cdot\absolutevalue{\beta}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\absolutevalue{\beta}\leq(\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}D^{\frac{D}{2}})^{-\frac{1}{D+1}}\absolutevalue{z}^{\frac{1}{D+1}},

so

\tilde{\lambda}\leq 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}D^{\frac{D}{2}}\absolutevalue{\beta}^{D}\leq 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}(\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}D^{\frac{D}{2}})^{\frac{1}{D+1}}\absolutevalue{z}^{\frac{D}{D+1}}\leq 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}D^{\frac{D}{2(D+1)}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{2(D+1)}}\absolutevalue{z}^{\frac{D}{D+1}}.

Furthermore, we also have

\absolutevalue{z}\geq\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot(\sqrt{D}\absolutevalue{\beta})^{D}\cdot\absolutevalue{\beta}\geq\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{D+1}D^{-\frac{1}{2}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq D^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{-(D+1)}\absolutevalue{z}.

Plugging this into the previous inequality, we find that

\tilde{\lambda}\leq 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}D^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{-1}\absolutevalue{z}.

∎

Lemma A.8

(Comparison)
.

Consider
(
39
)
and suppose that
\absolutevalue{z_j}\geq\absolutevalue{z_k}
.
Then, if
\theta_{j}(t_{0})\geq\theta_{k}(t_{0})
for some
t_{0}\geq 0
, we have
\theta_{j}(t)\geq\theta_{k}(t)
for all
t\geq t_{0}
.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
A.4
and we note that the initialization of
b_{j}(0)=b_{0}
is the same for all
j
.
∎

Lemma A.9

(Noise case)
.

For the gradient flow
(
39
)
,
suppose that
\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq b_{0}/\sqrt{D}
.
Denoting
{T}_{j}^{(1)}=\left(2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}b_{0}^{D}\absolutevalue{z_j}\right)^{-1}
,
we have

\theta_{j}(t)b_{j}^{D}(t)\leq 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{D},\quad\mbox{for}\quad t\leq{T}_{j}^{(1)}.

(41)

and

\theta_{j}(t)b_{j}^{D}(t)\leq 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}}\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{D}\exp( 2^{\frac{D+1}{2}} b_0^D \abs{z_j} (t- {T}_j^{(1)})^+),\quad\mbox{for}\quad t\leq\left(1+\log\frac{b_{0}}{\sqrt{D}\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right){T}_{j}^{(1)}.

(42)

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the proof of Lemma D.2 in
[
34
]
.
∎

Lemma A.10

(Signal case)
.

For the gradient flow
(
39
)
, denote

T^{\mathrm{eig}}_{j}=\inf{\left\{t\geq 0:\theta_{j}(t)b_{j}^{D}(t)\geq\absolutevalue{z_j}^{\frac{D+1}{D+2}}\right\}}.

We have

•

If
\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq b_{0}/\sqrt{D}
, then

\displaystyle T^{\mathrm{eig}}_{j}\leq 2(b_{0}^{D}\absolutevalue{z_j})^{-1}\left[1+\log^{+}\frac{(D^{-\frac{D}{2}}\absolutevalue{z_j}/2)^{\frac{1}{D+2}}}{\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right],

(43)

•

If
\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq b_{0}/\sqrt{D}
, then

\displaystyle T^{\mathrm{eig}}_{j}\leq 2\left(\sqrt{D}\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{D-1}\absolutevalue{z_j}\right)^{-1}\left(1+R_{j}\right),

(44)

where

\displaystyle R_{j}=\begin{cases}\log^{+}\frac{(D\absolutevalue{z_j}/2)^{\frac{1}{D+2}}}{b_{0}},&D=1,\\
\frac{1}{D-1},&D>1.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let us define
T^{\mathrm{sig}}_{j}=\inf{\left\{t\geq 0:\absolutevalue{f_j(t)}\geq\absolutevalue{z_j}/2\right\}}.

Using the conservation quantity, we find that
\theta_{j}(t)\geq\absolutevalue{\beta_j(t)}
and
b_{j}(t)\geq\sqrt{D}\absolutevalue{\beta_j(t)}
,
so

\absolutevalue{f_j(t)}=\absolutevalue{\theta_j(t) b_j^D(t) \beta_j(t)}\leq\absolutevalue{\theta_j(t) b_j^D(t)}^{\frac{D+2}{D+1}}

and thus
T^{\mathrm{eig}}_{j}\leq T^{\mathrm{sig}}_{j}
.
Now the rest follows from Lemma D.3 in
[
34
]
.
∎

A.5.1
Proof of Theorem
2.3

The proof is similar to the proof for the two-layer case in Subsection
A.4.1
,
but we have to consider in addition the interaction of the
b_{j}(t)
terms.
We recall that
b_{0}=b_{0}(n)\asymp n^{-\frac{1}{2(D+2)}}
and
t\leq t_{*}\asymp b_{0}^{-D}\sqrt{n/(\log n)}\asymp n^{\frac{D+1}{D+2}}/\sqrt{\log n}
.
Let us define

J=\min{\left\{j\geq 1:\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq b_{0}/\sqrt{D}\right\}}\asymp b_{0}^{-2/\gamma}\asymp n^{\frac{1}{(D+2)\gamma}}.

Following the proof of Subsection
A.4.1
, we can compute that

\delta^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\tilde{\lambda}})\geq\delta_{0}\asymp b_{0}^{2D}n^{-\frac{q\gamma}{p+q}}\asymp n^{-\frac{D}{D+2}}n^{-\frac{q\gamma}{p+q}}

Being the same as the proof in Subsection
A.4.1
,
we consider
j<L
and
j\geq L
separately.
For
j<L
, we can still apply the comparison principle Lemma
A.8
to show that the up-crossing can not happen.

Let us now consider the case
j\geq L
and prove that
\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)<\delta_{0}
.
For
j\geq\max(L,J)
, using
\absolutevalue{z_j}\lesssim\sqrt{\log(jn)/n}
and
t\leq t_{*}
, we find that

b_{0}^{D}\absolutevalue{z_j}t\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(jn)}{\log n}}\lesssim 1+\sqrt{\log j/\log n}.

Then, we apply (
42
) to get

\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)\lesssim b_{0}^{2D}\lambda_{j}\exp( C b_0^D \abs{z_j} t)\leq b_{0}^{2D}\lambda_{j}\exp( C \sqrt{\log j})<\delta_{0}=b_{0}^{2D}n^{-\frac{q\gamma}{p+q}},

but we have to verify the condition in (
42
).
Let us be more detailed here.
Since
j\geq J
, we can write

\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}=\kappa b_{0}/\sqrt{D},\quad\kappa\leq 1,\quad\log j\lesssim\log C(\kappa b_{0}/\sqrt{D})^{-2/\gamma}\lesssim 1+\log\kappa^{-1}+\log n.

On one hand, we have

t/{{T}_{j}^{(1)}}=Cb_{0}^{D}\absolutevalue{z_j}t\lesssim 1+\sqrt{\log j/\log n}\lesssim 1+\sqrt{\log\kappa^{-1}/\log n},

while on the other hand, we have

1+\log\frac{b_{0}}{\sqrt{D}\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}}=1+\log\kappa^{-1}\gtrsim 1+\sqrt{\log\kappa^{-1}/\log n}.

Therefore, taking the constant factor in
t
small, this condition is satisfied.

Now, it remains to consider the case that
J>L
and
j\in[L,J]
.
Applying Proposition
A.7
, we find

\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)\lesssim\max(\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{D},\absolutevalue{z_j}^{\frac{D+1}{D+2}},b_{0}^{-1}\absolutevalue{z_j})^{2}.

The first term already satisfies
\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}b_{0}^{D}\ll\delta_{0}
as considered before.
To control the second and the third term, we use
J>L
to get

n^{\frac{1}{(D+2)\gamma}}\gtrsim(n/\log n)^{\frac{q}{p+1}},\quad\Longrightarrow\quad n^{\frac{q\gamma}{p+1}}\lesssim(\log n)^{\frac{q\gamma}{p+1}}n^{\frac{1}{D+2}},

so using
q>1
, there is some
s>0
such that

\delta_{0}=b_{0}^{2D}n^{-\frac{q\gamma}{p+q}}\gtrsim b_{0}^{2D}n^{-\frac{1}{D+2}}n^{s}\asymp n^{-\frac{D+1}{D+2}}n^{s}.

Returning to the quantities, since
J
is still polynomial in
n
, we have
\absolutevalue{z_j}\lesssim\sqrt{\log(jn)/n}\lesssim\sqrt{(\log n)/n}
,
so

\absolutevalue{z_j}^{\frac{2(D+1)}{D+2}}\lesssim n^{-\frac{D+1}{D+2}}(\log n)^{\frac{D+1}{D+2}}\ll n^{-\frac{D+1}{D+2}}n^{s},

and

(b_{0}^{-1}\absolutevalue{z_j})^{2}\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{D+2}}n^{-1}\log n=n^{-\frac{D+1}{D+2}}\log n\ll n^{-\frac{D+1}{D+2}}n^{s}.

Therefore, we conclude in this case that
\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)<\delta_{0}
.

Finally, for the feature error measure, we can follow the same argument as in Subsection
A.4.1
except that we apply Lemma
A.9
and Lemma
A.10
.

The feature error measure

Now, let us consider the feature error measure at time
t=t_{*}
.
Let us choose
\delta_{*}=Cn^{-\frac{D+1}{2(D+2)}}
.
We first consider
\mathcal{E}_{\text{V}}(\delta^{*},n^{-1};\bm{\tilde{\lambda}})
.
For
j\geq L
, as discussed before, we have
\tilde{\lambda}_{j}<\delta_{0}\leq\delta_{*}
.
Moreover, for
j<L
such that
\absolutevalue{f^*_j}\lesssim\sqrt{(\log n)/n}
and
\lambda_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq b_{0}/\sqrt{D}
, we apply Lemma
A.9
to find that
\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t_{*})\leq C\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(0)
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\#{\left\{j\in N:\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t_{*})\geq\delta_{*}\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq\#{\left\{j<L:\absolutevalue{f^*_j}\gtrsim(n\log n)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}}+\#{\left\{j<L:\lambda_{j}\geq b_{0}/\sqrt{D}\right\}}+\#{\left\{j<L:C\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(0)\geq\delta_{*}\right\}}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{p+1}}+n^{\frac{1}{(D+2)\gamma}}

Now, for
\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}(\delta^{*},n^{-1};\bm{\tilde{\lambda}})
, we use Lemma
A.10
to find that if
\absolutevalue{f^*_j}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\log n)^{3/2}
, then

\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)\geq\absolutevalue{f^*_j}^{\frac{D+1}{D+2}}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{D+1}{2(D+2)}}(\log n)^{\frac{3}{2}\frac{D+1}{D+2}}\geq\delta_{*},

so

\sum_{j\in N}(f_{j}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t_{*})\geq\delta_{*}\right\}}\leq\sum_{j\in N}(f_{j}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\absolutevalue{f^*_j}\gtrsim n^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log n)^{3/2}\right\}}\lesssim n^{-\frac{p}{p+1}}(\log n)^{\frac{2p}{p+1}}.

Combining the two parts, we prove the feature error measure bound.

Appendix B
Proof for Diagonal Adaptive Kernel under Empirical Loss

B.1
Over-parameterized linear regression

Let us consider the over-parameterized high-dimensional linear regression.
Let us denote by
S
the indices of the signals and
R=[d]\backslash S
the rest of the indices.
It is easy to see that the gradient flow equation can be given explicitly as

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\theta}&=\beta(t)\odot(w^{*}-w(t)+r(t)),\quad\theta(0)=\alpha\bm{1},\\
\dot{\beta}&=\theta(t)\odot(w^{*}-w(t)+r(t)),\quad\beta(0)=\bm{0},\end{aligned}\right.

where
w(t)=\theta(t)\odot\beta(t)
,

r(t)=(\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d})(w^{*}-w(t))+h,\quad\hat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{{\top}},\quad h=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}x_{i}.

B.1.1
Proof of Theorem
3.1

First, we bound the perturbation term
r(t)
.
We decompose
r_{j}
as

\displaystyle r_{j}

\displaystyle=(\hat{\Sigma}_{j\cdot}-I_{j\cdot})(w^{*}-w(t))+h=(\hat{\Sigma}_{jS}-I_{jS})(w^{*}_{S}-w_{S}(t))+(\hat{\Sigma}_{jR}-I_{jR})(w^{*}_{R}-w_{R}(t))+h_{j}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon r_{j,1}+r_{j,2}+h_{j}.

For
h_{j}
, standard sub-Gaussian concentration inequality gives that with probability at least
1-Cd^{-2}
,

\absolutevalue{h_j}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n}},\quad\forall j\in[d].

For
r_{j,1}
, sub-exponential concentration also gives that with probability at least
1-Cd^{-2}
,

\norm{\hat{\Sigma}_{j S} - I_{j S}}_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{s^{*}\log d}{n}},\quad\forall j\in[d].

Therefore, as
s^{*}
is a constant, we have

\absolutevalue{r_{j,1}}\leq\norm{\hat{\Sigma}_{j S} - I_{j S}}_{2}\norm{w^*_S - w_S(t)}_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{s^{*}\log d}{n}}\norm{w^*_S - w_S(t)}_{2}.

For
r_{j,2}
, we notice that
w^{*}_{R}=0
, so

\absolutevalue{r_{j,2}}=\absolutevalue{(\hat{\Sigma}_{j R } - I_{j R}) w_R(t)}\leq\norm{\hat{\Sigma}_{j R } - I_{j R}}_{2}\norm{w_R(t)}_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\norm{\hat{\Sigma}_{j R } - I_{j R}}_{2}\norm{w_R(t)}_{\infty}.

Standard concentration inequality also gives that with probability at least
1-Cd^{-2}
,

\norm{\hat{\Sigma}_{j R} - I_{j R}}_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d\log d}{n}},\quad\forall j\in[d].

Consequently,

\absolutevalue{r_{j,2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d^{2}\log d}{n}}\norm{w_R(t)}_{\infty}.

In the following, we claim that there is a constant
C_{0}>0
such that for
t\in[0,t_{*}]
,

\norm{w^*_S - w_S(t)}_{2}\leq C_{0},\qquad\norm{w_R(t)}_{\infty}\leq C_{0}d^{-1}.

(45)

With this claim, the bound of the three terms in
r_{j}
sum up to

\absolutevalue{r_j(t)}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}},\quad\forall j\in[d].

(46)

Using the bound of
r_{j}(t)
, we can follow the same lines of proof in Subsection
A.3
to show
the monotonicity of the feature error measure and its final value.
Particularly, we use the following fact: for
j\in S
, we have

\absolutevalue{w^*_j + r_j(t)}\geq\absolutevalue{w^*_j}-\absolutevalue{r_j(t)}\geq\absolutevalue{w^*_j}-C\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{w^*_j}\geq c

while for
j\in R
, we have

\absolutevalue{w^*_j + r_j(t)}\leq\absolutevalue{r_j(t)}\leq C\sqrt{\frac{\log(d)}{n}}.

Proof of the claim.

Now, let us prove the claim in (
45
).
Since
(\log d)(\log n)=0(n)
, the perturbation term can be written as

\absolutevalue{r_j}\leq CC_{0}\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}}=\eta C_{0},

where
\eta>0
can be taken such that
\eta\log n
is arbitrary small.
Now, by taking
C_{0}=2+\norm{w^*_S}
being a constant, (
45
) holds with strict inequality when
t=0
,
so we can let

t_{0}=\inf{\left\{t\in[0,t_{*}]:\norm{w^*_S - w_S(t)}_{2}=C_{0}\quad\mbox{or}\quad\norm{w_R(t)}_{\infty}=C_{0}d^{-1}\right\}}>0.

We will prove by contradiction that
t_{0}=t_{*}
.

On one hand, for
j\in S
,

\dot{w}_{j}(t)=(\theta_{j}^{2}(t)+\beta_{j}^{2}(t))(w^{*}_{j}-w_{j}(t)+r_{j}(t)).

As long as
\eta\leq\min_{j\in S}\absolutevalue{w^*_j}/(2C_{0})
, we have

\absolutevalue{r_j(t)}\leq\eta C_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{w^*_j},

so if
\absolutevalue{w_j(t_0) - w^*_j}\geq\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{w^*_j}
, the sign of
\dot{w}_{j}(t_{0})
must be the same as that of
w^{*}_{j}-w_{j}(t_{0})
,
which implies that
\absolutevalue{w_j(t_0) - w^*_j}
must be non-increasing at
t_{0}
.
In addition, if we have
\absolutevalue{w_j(t_0) - w^*_j}<\frac{1}{2}\absolutevalue{w^*_j}
for all
j\in S
, we already have
\norm{w^*_S - w_S(t_0)}_{2}\leq\norm{w^*_S}/2<C_{0}
.
Therefore, it can not be the case that
\norm{w^*_S - w_S(t_0)}_{2}=C_{0}
.

On the other hand, for
j\in R
, Lemma
A.5
shows that
we have

\absolutevalue{w_j(t)}\leq\sqrt{2}\alpha^{2}\leq\sqrt{2}d^{-1},\quad\mbox{for}\quad t\leq\min(T,t_{0}),

where

T=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}M},\quad M=\max_{t\leq t_{0}}\absolutevalue{w^*_j + r_j(t)}=\max_{t\leq t_{0}}\absolutevalue{r_j(t)}\leq\eta C_{0}

Consequently, we can choose
\eta
small enough that
T\geq t_{*}\asymp\log n
.
Hence,
\absolutevalue{w_j(t_0)}\leq\sqrt{2}d^{-1}<C_{0}d^{-1}
,
so
\norm{w_R(t_0)}_{\infty}=C_{0}d^{-1}
is also impossible.
Combining the two cases, we find that
t_{0}=t_{*}
, which completes the proof of the claim.

B.2
Diagonal adaptive kernel

Let us consider the general version of the theorem in the following.
To describe the properties of the truth function with the greatest generality, let us introduce the following quantities on the truth coefficients
(f_{j}^{*})_{j\geq 1}
, which is also introduced in
[
33
]
:

\mathcal{N}(\delta;f^{*})\coloneqq\#{\left\{j:\absolutevalue{f_{j}^*}\geq\delta\right\}},\quad\mathcal{R}(\delta;f^{*})\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(f_{j}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}\{\absolutevalue{f_j^*}<\delta\}.

(47)

These two quantities measure the number of significant coefficients and the sum of residual terms of the truth function, respectively.
Moreover, they can be viewed as the optimal feature error measure for the truth function, where the weights of the feature map are in line with the truth coefficients.
We note that since
\absolutevalue{f_{j}^*}
may not be decreasing in
j
(for instance, consider Assumption
1
),
so these two quantities are not simply obtained by partitioning first
L
terms and the rest.
Under Assumption
1
, we have

\mathcal{N}(\delta;f^{*})\asymp\delta^{-\frac{2}{p+1}}
and
\mathcal{R}(\delta;f^{*})\asymp\delta^{p}
.

We also have to make the following mild assumption on the truth coefficients,
which requires that the span of the significant coefficients is not exponentially large
and the significant coefficients decay fast enough that they are summable.
They are satisfied when Assumption
1
holds.

Assumption 8
.

There exists constant
B_{\infty}
such that
\absolutevalue{f_j^*}\leq B_{\infty}
for all
j\geq 1
.
Moreover, there are constants
\kappa\geq 1,B_{\mathrm{spn}},s_{0},B_{\mathrm{sig}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\max{\left\{j:\absolutevalue{\theta_{j}^*}\geq\delta\right\}}\leq B_{\mathrm{spn}}\delta^{-\kappa},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{R}(\delta;f^{*})\leq B_{\mathrm{sig}}\delta^{-(1-s_{0})},\quad\forall\delta>0.

(48)

Then, Theorem
3.2
is a direct consequence of the following two theorems.

Theorem B.1
.

Consider the diagonal adaptive kernel method in
(
12
)
with the empirical loss
\mathcal{L}_{n}
under Assumption
7
and Assumption
8
.
Let
s>0
be an arbitrary small constant.
Then, we can choose
L\asymp(-\frac{1}{2}+s)\log n
,
a decreasing sequence
\delta_{l}=C2^{-l}
for
l\leq L
satisfying
\delta_{L}\leq n^{-\frac{1}{2}+s}
,
and times
t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{L}=t_{*}\lesssim\sqrt{n}
satisfying
t_{l}\lesssim\delta_{l}^{-l}\log n
, such that,
with probability at least
1-Cn^{-2}
, we have

\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\Phi_{\bm{\theta}(t)},f^{*})\leq\mathcal{R}(\delta_{l};f^{*})+\left(\mathcal{N}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}};f^{*})+n^{\frac{1}{2\gamma}}\right)n^{-1},\quad\forall t\in[t_{l},t_{*}],~\forall l=0,\dots,L,

(49)

and in particular,

\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\Phi_{\bm{\theta}(t_{*})},f^{*})\leq\mathcal{R}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}+s};f^{*})+\left(\mathcal{N}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}};f^{*})+n^{\frac{1}{2\gamma}}\right)n^{-1}

Additionally, we notice that the upper bound in
(
49
)
is monotonically decreasing in
l
.

Theorem B.2
.

Consider the diagonal adaptive kernel method in
(
15
)
with the empirical loss
\mathcal{L}_{n}
under Assumption
7
and Assumption
8
,
where we choose
b_{0}\asymp n^{-\frac{1}{2(D+2)}}
.
Let
s>0
be an arbitrary small constant.
Denote
q=2^{\frac{2(D+1)}{D+2}}
.
Then, we can choose
L\asymp(-\frac{1}{2}+s)\log n
,
a decreasing sequence
\delta_{l}=Cq^{-l}
for
l\leq L
satisfying
\delta_{L}\leq n^{-\frac{1}{2}+s}
,
and times
t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{L}=t_{*}\lesssim n^{\frac{D+1}{D+2}}
satisfying
t_{l}\lesssim\delta_{l}^{-l}\log n
, such that,
with probability at least
1-Cn^{-2}
, we have

\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\Phi_{\bm{\theta}(t),\bm{b}(t)},f^{*})\leq\mathcal{R}(\delta_{l};f^{*})+\left(\mathcal{N}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}};f^{*})+n^{\frac{1+s}{(D+2)\gamma}}\right)n^{-1},\quad\forall t\in[t_{l},t_{*}],~\forall l=0,\dots,L.

(50)

In particular,

\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\Phi_{\bm{\theta}(t_{*}),\bm{b}(t_{*})},f^{*})\leq\mathcal{R}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}+s};f^{*})+\left(\mathcal{N}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}};f^{*})+n^{\frac{1+s}{(D+2)\gamma}}\right)n^{-1}.

B.2.1
Proof of Theorem
B.1

The proof relies on investigating the proof of Theorem 1 in
[
33
]
.
Denote

S=S_{1}\cup S_{2}={\left\{j\geq 1:\absolutevalue{\theta^*_j}\geq n^{-1/2}\sqrt{\ln n}\right\}}\cup{\left\{j\geq 1:\lambda_{j}\geq n^{-1/2}\right\}}

and
R=S^{\complement}
as in
[
33
]
.
Let
\nu_{1}=Cn^{-1/2+s}
and
L=\lfloor\log_{2}(B_{\infty}/\nu_{1})\rfloor
, where
s>0
is an arbitrary small constant.
The shrinkage dynamics in the proof of
[
33
, Theorem 1]
shows that,
letting
\delta_{i}=2^{-i}B_{\infty}
, there is a sequence of increasing times
t_{i}
such that,
t_{i}\lesssim\delta_{i}^{-1}\log n
and for
i=0,\dots,L
,

\norm{\bm{f}^*_S - \bm{f}_S(t)}_{\infty}\leq\delta_{i+1},\quad\forall t\geq t_{i+1}.

(51)

Also, we have
\delta_{I+1}\lesssim n^{-1/2+s}
and
t_{*}=t_{L}\lesssim\sqrt{n}
.
Therefore, we have

\theta_{k}(t_{i})^{2}\geq\absolutevalue{f_k(t_i)}\geq\absolutevalue{f_k^*}-\norm{\bm{f}^*_S - \bm{f}_S(t_i)}_{\infty}\geq\absolutevalue{f_k^*}-\delta_{i}\geq\delta_{i},\quad\forall k,\absolutevalue{f_k^*}\geq 2\delta_{i}.

(52)

On the other hand, the noise terms are bounded by

\absolutevalue{f_k(t)}\leq 2\lambda_{k}\exp(C \sqrt{\ln n + \ln k})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\absolutevalue{\theta_k(t)}^{2}\leq C\lambda_{k}\exp(C \sqrt{\ln n + \ln k})\leq n^{-1/2+s},\quad\forall k\in R,~t\leq t_{*},

where
s>0
is an arbitrary small constant.

Now let us consider the feature error measure.
We have

\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t),f^{*})\leq\mathcal{E}(\delta_{i},n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t))=\#{\left\{k\in N:\theta_{k}(t)^{2}\geq\delta_{i}\right\}}n^{-1}+\sum_{k\in N}(f_{k}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\theta_{k}(t)^{2}<\delta_{i}\right\}}.

For the first term, using the control of the noise terms and that
\delta_{i}\geq Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}+s}
,
we find that for
t\leq t_{*}
,

\#{\left\{k\in N:\theta_{k}(t)^{2}\geq\delta_{i}\right\}}\leq\absolutevalue{S}\leq\mathcal{N}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}};f^{*})+n^{\frac{1}{2\gamma}}

For the second term, we use (
52
) to get

\sum_{k\in N}(f_{k}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\theta_{k}(t)^{2}<\delta_{i}\right\}}\leq\sum_{k\in N}(f_{k}^{*})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\absolutevalue{f_k^*}<2\delta_{i}\right\}}=\mathcal{R}(2\delta_{i};f^{*}),\quad\forall t\in[t_{i},t_{*}].

Combining the two estimates, we obtain the bound on
\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t_{i}),f^{*})
.
Particularly, using
\delta_{L}\lesssim n^{-1/2+s}
, we have

\mathcal{E}^{*}(n^{-1};\bm{\lambda}(t_{*}),f^{*})\leq\mathcal{R}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}+s};f^{*})+\left(\mathcal{N}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}+s};f^{*})+n^{\frac{1+s}{2\gamma}}\right)n^{-1}.

B.2.2
Proof of Theorem
B.2

The proof is similar to the proof for the two-layer case in Subsection
B.2.1

and we follow the proof of Theorem 2 in
[
33
]
here.
We denote
\tilde{\lambda}_{j}(t)=(\theta_{j}(t)b_{j}^{D}(t))^{2}
.
In this case, for some small
s^{\prime}>0
, we denote

S=S_{1}\cup S_{2}={\left\{j\geq 1:\absolutevalue{f^*_j}\geq n^{-1/2}\sqrt{\ln n}\right\}}\cup{\left\{j\geq 1:\lambda_{j}\geq n^{-\frac{1+s^{\prime}}{D+2}}\right\}}.

Let us still define
\nu_{1}=Cn^{-\frac{1}{2}+s_{1}}
,
L=\lfloor\log_{2}(B_{\infty}/\nu_{1})\rfloor

and
\delta_{i}=2^{-i}B_{\infty}
.
The shrinkage dynamics shows that we have a sequence of increasing times
t_{i}
such that (
51
) holds.
Moreover, we have

t_{i}\lesssim\sum_{j\leq i}\left[\delta_{j}^{-\frac{2D+2}{D+2}}+\delta_{j}^{-1}b_{0}^{-D}\log n\right]\lesssim\delta_{i}^{-\frac{2D+2}{D+2}}+\delta_{i}^{-1}b_{0}^{-D}\log n\lesssim\delta_{i}^{-1}b_{0}^{-D}\log n\asymp\delta_{i}^{-1}n^{\frac{D}{2(D+2)}}\log n,

where the last inequality follows from that
\delta_{i}\gtrsim n^{-\frac{1}{2}+s_{1}}
so the second term dominates.
Regarding the multilayer case, we have
t_{L}\lesssim n^{\frac{D+1}{D+2}}
and

\tilde{\lambda}_{k}(t_{i})\geq\absolutevalue{f_k(t_i)}^{\frac{2(D+1)}{D+2}}\geq\delta_{i}^{\frac{2(D+1)}{D+2}},\quad\forall k,\absolutevalue{f_k^*}\geq 2\delta_{i}.

The noise terms are bounded by

\absolutevalue{\tilde{\lambda}_k(t)}\lesssim\lambda_{k}b_{0}^{2D}\exp(E \sqrt{\ln n + \ln k})\lesssim n^{-\frac{D+1+s^{\prime}}{D+2}}.

Consequently, the control of the feature error measure follows the same argument as in Subsection
B.2.1
.

Appendix C
Hermite Polynomials and Gaussian Distribution

In this section, we collect some useful properties of the Hermite polynomials and also the Gaussian distribution.
Let us denote by
\gamma_{d}
the standard Gaussian measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We denote by
H_{r}
the normalized Hermite polynomials with respect to the standard Gaussian measure
\gamma_{1}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim N(0,1)}H_{r}(x)H_{s}(x)=\delta_{rs}
.
It is known that the Hermite polynomials
(H_{r})_{r\geq 0}
form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\gamma_{1})
.
Moreover, the generating function of the Hermite polynomials is given by

\exp(xt - \frac{t^2}{2})=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{r}(x)}{\sqrt{r!}}t^{r}.

(53)

For a multi-index
{\bm{m}}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{d})
, we define the tensorized Hermite polynomial
H_{{\bm{m}}}=\prod_{j=1}^{d}H_{m_{j}}
.
Then, the set of tensorized Hermite polynomials
\{H_{{\bm{m}}}:{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\}
forms an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\gamma_{d})
.
The generating function of the multi-index Hermite polynomials is given by

\displaystyle\exp(\ang{x,t} - \frac{1}{2} \norm{t}^2)=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\frac{H_{{\bm{m}}}(x)}{\sqrt{{\bm{m}}!}}t^{{\bm{m}}},

where we use the convention
{\bm{m}}!=\prod_{i=1}^{p}m_{i}!
and
t^{{\bm{m}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}t_{i}^{m_{i}}
.
Let
{\bm{m}}
,
{\bm{n}}
be multi-indices in
\mathbb{N}^{p}
.
We denote the multi-index combinatorial by
\binom{{\bm{m}}}{{\bm{n}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{m_{i}}{n_{i}}.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, and let
P\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
and
Q\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,q)
be Stiefel matrices.
Let
{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
and
{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
be multi-indices.
We have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}\left[H_{\bm{m}}(P^{\top}x)H_{\bm{n}}(Q^{\top}x)\right]&=\sqrt{{\bm{m}}!{\bm{n}}!}[\alpha^{\bm{m}}\beta^{\bm{n}}]\exp(\alpha^\top R \beta)=\delta_{|{\bm{m}}|,|{\bm{n}}|}\sum_{\Upsilon\in\Pi({\bm{m}},{\bm{n}})}\frac{\sqrt{{\bm{m}}!{\bm{n}}!}}{\Upsilon!}R^{\Upsilon}\end{aligned}

(54)

where
R=P^{\top}Q\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
,
\Pi({\bm{m}},{\bm{n}})\subset\mathbb{N}^{p\times q}
is the set of integer matrices
\Upsilon=[\Upsilon_{i,j}]
satisfying

\sum_{j=1}^{q}\Upsilon_{i,j}=m_{i}\quad\text{for all }i,\quad\sum_{i=1}^{p}\Upsilon_{i,j}=n_{j}\quad\text{for all }j.

and
\Upsilon!=\prod_{i,j}\Upsilon_{i,j}!
,
R^{\Upsilon}=\prod_{i,j}(R_{i,j})^{\Upsilon_{i,j}}
.

Proof.

Let
u=P^{\top}x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
v=Q^{\top}x\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
. Then
(u,v)
is jointly Gaussian with mean
0
and covariance matrix

\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}I_{p}&R\\
R^{\top}&I_{q}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{where }R=P^{\top}Q\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}.

For the pair
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
, consider the joint generating function:

\exp\big(\langle\alpha, u \rangle+ \langle\beta, v \rangle\big)=\exp\big(\alpha^\top P^\top x + \beta^\top Q^\top x\big)=\exp\big(\langle P \alpha+ Q \beta, x \rangle\big).

Since
x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, the expectation of the exponential is:

\mathbb{E}\big[\exp(\langle P \alpha+ Q \beta, x \rangle)\big]=\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2} \|P \alpha+ Q \beta\|^2\Big).

Expanding the quadratic term:

\|P\alpha+Q\beta\|^{2}=\alpha^{\top}P^{\top}P\alpha+\beta^{\top}Q^{\top}Q\beta+2\alpha^{\top}P^{\top}Q\beta=\|\alpha\|^{2}+\|\beta\|^{2}+2\alpha^{\top}R\beta,

since
P^{\top}P=I_{p}
and
Q^{\top}Q=I_{q}
. Thus,

\mathbb{E}\big[\exp(\langle\alpha, u \rangle+ \langle\beta, v \rangle)\big]=\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2} \|\alpha\|^2 + \frac{1}{2} \|\beta\|^2 + \alpha^\top R \beta\Big).

Expanding the left-hand side:

\exp\big(\langle\alpha, u \rangle+ \langle\beta, v \rangle\big)=\exp(\frac{1}{2}\norm{\alpha}^2 + \frac{1}{2} \norm{\beta}^2 )\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\frac{\alpha^{\bm{m}}}{\sqrt{{\bm{m}}!}}H_{\bm{m}}(u)\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}}\frac{\beta^{\bm{n}}}{\sqrt{{\bm{n}}!}}H_{\bm{n}}(v).

Taking the expectation:

\mathbb{E}\big[\exp(\langle\alpha, u \rangle+ \langle\beta, v \rangle)\big]=\exp(\frac{1}{2}\norm{\alpha}^2 + \frac{1}{2} \norm{\beta}^2 )\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}}\frac{\alpha^{\bm{m}}}{\sqrt{{\bm{m}}!}}\frac{\beta^{\bm{n}}}{\sqrt{{\bm{n}}!}}\mathbb{E}\big[H_{\bm{m}}(u)H_{\bm{n}}(v)\big].

Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{q}}\frac{\alpha^{\bm{m}}\beta^{\bm{n}}}{\sqrt{{\bm{m}}!{\bm{n}}!}}\mathbb{E}\big[H_{\bm{m}}(u)H_{\bm{n}}(v)\big]=\exp(\alpha^\top R \beta)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\big(\alpha^{\top}R\beta\big)^{k}

Matching the coefficients, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[H_{\bm{m}}(u)H_{\bm{n}}(v)\big]=\sqrt{{\bm{m}}!{\bm{n}}!}[\alpha^{\bm{m}}\beta^{\bm{n}}]\exp(\alpha^\top R \beta).

It remains to prove the last expression.
As
\alpha^{{\top}}R\beta=\sum_{i,j}R_{i,j}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\alpha^{{\top}}R\beta\right)^{k}=\sum_{\Upsilon:\sum_{ij}\Upsilon_{ij}=k}\frac{k!}{\prod_{i,j}\Upsilon_{ij}!}\prod_{i,j}R_{i,j}^{\Upsilon_{ij}}\alpha_{i}^{\Upsilon_{ij}}\beta_{j}^{\Upsilon_{ij}},

so

\displaystyle\exp(\alpha^\top R \beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\Upsilon:\sum_{ij}\Upsilon_{ij}=k}\frac{1}{\prod_{i,j}\Upsilon_{ij}!}\prod_{i,j}R_{i,j}^{\Upsilon_{ij}}\alpha_{i}^{\Upsilon_{ij}}\beta_{j}^{\Upsilon_{ij}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\Upsilon\in\mathbb{N}^{p\times q}}\frac{1}{\Upsilon!}\prod_{i,j}R_{i,j}^{\Upsilon_{ij}}\alpha_{i}^{\Upsilon_{ij}}\beta_{j}^{\Upsilon_{ij}}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle[\alpha^{\bm{m}}\beta^{\bm{n}}]\exp(\alpha^\top R \beta)=\sum_{\Upsilon\in\mathbb{N}^{p\times q},\sum_{j}\Upsilon_{ij}=m_{i},\sum_{i}\Upsilon_{ij}=n_{j}}\frac{1}{\Upsilon!}\prod_{i,j}R_{i,j}^{\Upsilon_{ij}}

∎

Corollary C.2
.

Let
x\sim N(0,I_{d})
and
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}H_{m}(\left\langle{u,x}\right\rangle)H_{n}(\left\langle{v,x}\right\rangle)=\delta_{m,n}\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{m},

(55)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}H_{m}(\left\langle{u,x}\right\rangle)H_{{\bm{n}}}(x)=\delta_{m,\absolutevalue{\nn}}\binom{m}{{\bm{n}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{{\bm{n}}}.

(56)

Proof.

The first identity follows easily from Lemma
C.1
by taking
P=u
and
Q=v
,
where
p=q=1
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.

For the second identity, let us take
P=u
and
Q=I_{d}
in Lemma
C.1
,
so
p=1,q=d
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}H_{m}(\left\langle{u,x}\right\rangle)H_{{\bm{n}}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{m!{\bm{n}}!}[\alpha^{m}\beta^{{\bm{n}}}]\exp(\alpha^\top u^\T\beta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{m!{\bm{n}}!}[\alpha^{m}\beta^{{\bm{n}}}]\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{k!}(\alpha u^{\top}\beta)^{k}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{m!{\bm{n}}!}\delta_{m,\absolutevalue{\nn}}[\alpha^{m}\beta^{{\bm{n}}}]\frac{\alpha^{m}(u^{\top}\beta)^{m}}{m!}

\displaystyle=\delta_{m,\absolutevalue{\nn}}\sqrt{m!{\bm{n}}!}\frac{1}{m!}[\beta^{{\bm{n}}}](u^{\top}\beta)^{m}

\displaystyle=\delta_{m,\absolutevalue{\nn}}\sqrt{m!{\bm{n}}!}\frac{1}{m!}\binom{m}{{\bm{n}}}u^{{\bm{n}}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{m,\absolutevalue{\nn}}\binom{m}{{\bm{n}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}u^{{\bm{n}}}.

∎

Corollary C.3
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
.
The space
\mathcal{H}_{r}=\spn\{H_{{\bm{m}}}:{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p},\absolutevalue{\mm}=r\}
is invariant under
P_{Q}
for any
Q\in{\mathcal{S}}(p,p)
.

Lemma C.4
.

Let
\sigma\in[-1,1]
and
\xi\sim N(0,1-\sigma^{2})
.
Then,

\mathbb{E}_{\xi}H_{m}(\sigma y+\xi)=\sigma^{m}H_{m}(y).

Proof.

We prove the lemma using the generating function (
53
) of the normalized Hermite polynomials.
Let us define

g(t)=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\exp\left(t(\sigma y+\xi)-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\right).

Expanding the right hand side using (
53
), we obtain:

g(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\xi}H_{m}(\sigma y+\xi)}{\sqrt{m!}}t^{m}.

On the other hand, we can rewrite
g(t)
as

g(t)=\exp\left(t\sigma y-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\exp(t \xi)=\exp(t \sigma y - \frac{t^2 \sigma^2}{2})\exp\left(\frac{t^{2}(1-\sigma^{2})}{2}\right)=\exp\left(t\sigma y-\frac{t^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}\right).

Therefore, using (
53
) again, we have

g(t)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{m}(\sigma y)}{\sqrt{m!}}(t\sigma)^{m}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{m}(y)}{\sqrt{m!}}\sigma^{m}t^{m}.

Comparing the two expansions yields the desired result.
∎

The following is a standard result on the Hermite polynomials.

Lemma C.5

(Recurrence and Derivative)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We have

\sqrt{n+1}H_{n+1}=xH_{n}-\sqrt{n}H_{n-1}.

(57)

Moreover, for any multi-index
{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
,

\nabla_{x_{i}}H_{{\bm{m}}}(x)=\sqrt{m_{i}}H_{{\bm{m}}-e_{i}}(x),

(58)

where
e_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
is the
i
-th unit vector and we use the convention
H_{{\bm{m}}-e_{i}}=0
if
m_{i}=0
.

Let
f=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}f_{{\bm{m}}}H_{{\bm{m}}}
be the Hermite expansion of a function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then, with Lemma
C.5
, we have

\nabla_{x_{i}}f=\sum_{{\bm{m}}}f_{{\bm{m}}}\nabla_{x_{i}}H_{{\bm{m}}}=\sum_{{\bm{m}}}f_{{\bm{m}}}\sqrt{m_{i}}H_{{\bm{m}}-e_{i}}=\sum_{{\bm{m}}}\sqrt{m_{i}+1}f_{{\bm{m}}+e_{i}}H_{{\bm{m}}}.

(59)

C.1
Gaussian distribution

We will use the Gaussian integral by parts formula:

\mathbb{E}X_{i}h(X)=\mathbb{E}\partial_{i}h(X),\quad\mathbb{E}X_{i}X_{j}h(X)=\delta_{ij}\mathbb{E}h(X)+\mathbb{E}\partial_{ij}h(X).

Lemma C.6
.

Let
X\sim N(0,I_{d})
and
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
f,g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be twice differentiable functions such that their derivatives up to second order are square-integrable.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle{u,X}\right\rangle\left\langle{v,X}\right\rangle f(X)g(X)\right]

\displaystyle=\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle\mathbb{E}\left[f(X)g(X)\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle{u,\nabla f(X)}\right\rangle\left\langle{v,\nabla g(X)}\right\rangle\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle{v,\nabla f(X)}\right\rangle\left\langle{u,\nabla g(X)}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[f(X)u^{\top}\nabla^{2}g(X)v\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[g(X)v^{\top}\nabla^{2}f(X)u\right].

Proof.

Starting from
E[(u^{T}X)f(X)(v^{T}X)g(X)]
, we first express
(u^{T}X)(v^{T}X)
as
\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}u_{i}v_{j}X_{i}X_{j}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(u^{T}X)f(X)(v^{T}X)g(X)]=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}u_{i}v_{j}\mathbb{E}[X_{i}X_{j}f(X)g(X)].

Let us use the Gaussian integral by parts formula to get

\mathbb{E}X_{i}X_{j}f(X)g(X)=\delta_{ij}\mathbb{E}fg+\mathbb{E}\partial_{ij}(fg)=\delta_{ij}\mathbb{E}fg+\mathbb{E}\left(f_{ij}g+f_{i}g_{j}+f_{j}g_{i}+fg_{ij}\right),

where we denote by
f_{i},f_{ij},g_{i},g_{ij}
the derivatives of
f
and
g
.

Summing up the terms, we have

∎

Lemma C.7
.

Let
f,g
be smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that their derivatives up to third order are continuous and square-integrable with respect to the standard Gaussian measure
\gamma_{d}
.
Let
A,B
be two matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
Suppose that
A\nabla f=0
and
B\nabla g=0
,
Then, we have

\mathbb{E}[(\nabla f(X))^{\top}AX\cdot(\nabla g(X))^{\top}BX]=\mathbb{E}(\nabla f)^{{\top}}AB^{{\top}}\nabla g+\mathbb{E}\Tr(\nabla^2 f A \nabla^2 g B)

(60)

Proof.

Let
A=(a_{ij})
and
B=(b_{kl})
.
We denote by
f_{i},f_{ij},g_{i},\dots
the derivatives of
f
and
g
.
Then, we can expand the result as

I=\mathbb{E}[(\nabla f(X))^{\top}AX\cdot(\nabla g(X))^{\top}BX]=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ij}a_{ij}f_{i}x_{j}\sum_{kl}b_{kl}g_{k}x_{l}=\sum_{ijkl}a_{ij}b_{kl}\mathbb{E}x_{j}x_{l}(f_{i}g_{k})

Using the Gaussian integral by parts formula, we have

\mathbb{E}x_{j}x_{l}(f_{i}g_{k})=\delta_{jl}\mathbb{E}f_{i}g_{k}+\mathbb{E}\left(f_{ijl}h_{k}+f_{il}h_{jk}+f_{ij}h_{kl}+f_{i}h_{jkl}\right),

so we have

I=I_{0}+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}+I_{5}.

To simplify the result, the condition
A\nabla f=B\nabla g=0
writes

\sum_{j}a_{ij}f_{j}=0,\quad\sum_{l}b_{kl}g_{l}=0.

Consequently, since the derivatives are linear and communitative, we have

\displaystyle I_{1}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ijkl}a_{ij}b_{kl}f_{ijl}h_{k}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ikl}b_{kl}h_{k}\sum_{j}a_{ij}f_{jil}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ikl}b_{kl}h_{k}\partial_{il}\sum_{j}a_{ij}f_{j}=0.

Similarly,
I_{5}=0
.
In addition,

I_{3}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ijkl}a_{ij}b_{kl}f_{ij}h_{kl}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ikl}b_{kl}h_{kl}\sum_{j}a_{ij}f_{ji}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ikl}b_{kl}h_{kl}\partial_{i}\sum_{j}a_{ij}f_{j}=0.

For the remaining terms, we can write

\displaystyle I_{0}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ijkl}a_{ij}b_{kl}\delta_{jl}f_{i}g_{k}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ijk}a_{ij}b_{kj}f_{i}g_{k}=\mathbb{E}(\nabla f)^{{\top}}AB^{{\top}}\nabla g,

while

\displaystyle I_{2}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{ijkl}a_{ij}b_{kl}f_{il}h_{jk}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{l}\sum_{ijk}f_{li}a_{ij}h_{jk}b_{kl}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{l}(\nabla^{2}fA\nabla^{2}gB)_{ll}=\mathbb{E}\Tr(\nabla^2 f A \nabla^2 g B).

∎

Appendix D
Proof for the single-index model

In the following analysis, let us introduce
\rho=\left\langle{w,w_{*}}\right\rangle
as the cosine of the angle between
w
and
w_{*}
.
Moreover, the parameterization also gives that

f(x)=\left\langle{\bm{\beta},\Phi_{w}(x)}\right\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})}=\sum_{r\geq 0}\beta_{r}\lambda_{r}^{\frac{1}{2}}H_{r}(\left\langle{w,x}\right\rangle)=g(\left\langle{w,x}\right\rangle),

where the function
g
is defined by

g(u)=\sum_{r\geq 0}g_{r}H_{r}(u),\quad g_{r}=\lambda_{r}^{\frac{1}{2}}\beta_{r}.

We recall that the eigenvalues are taken as
\lambda_{r}\asymp\exp(-\gamma r)
.
Using Corollary
C.2
, we find that

f_{{\bm{m}}}=\left\langle{f,H_{{\bm{m}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\left\langle{\sum_{r\geq 0}g_{r}H_{r}(\left\langle{w,\cdot}\right\rangle),H_{{\bm{m}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}w^{{\bm{m}}}g_{r},\quad r=\absolutevalue{m}.

(61)

Regarding the projection on the sphere, we introduce the projection operator
P_{w}^{\perp}
as

P_{w}^{\perp}v=v-\left\langle{v,w}\right\rangle w
for
w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

D.1
Basic Properties of the Feature Error Measure

For the single index model, we can explicitly compute the feature error measure.
First, it is easy to see that

\mathcal{E}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w},f^{*})-\mathcal{E}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w_{*}},f^{*})=\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{w},f^{*}).

(62)

To compute the projection, recalling that

f^{*}(x)=g^{*}(\left\langle{w_{*},x}\right\rangle)=\sum_{r\geq 0}g^{*}_{r}H_{r}(\left\langle{w_{*},x}\right\rangle),

we define

f^{*}_{r,w}\coloneqq\left\langle{f^{*},H_{r}(\left\langle{w,\cdot}\right\rangle)}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\left\langle{\sum_{s\geq 0}g^{*}_{r}H_{s}(\left\langle{w_{*},x}\right\rangle),H_{r}(\left\langle{w,\cdot}\right\rangle)}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\left\langle{w,w_{*}}\right\rangle^{r}g^{*}_{r}=\rho^{r}g^{*}_{r},

(63)

where we apply Corollary
C.2
for the second equality.
Therefore, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{w},f^{*})=\sum_{r\geq 0}(1-\rho^{2r})(g^{*}_{r})^{2}.

(64)

Since
\rho\in[-1,1]
,
\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{w},f^{*})=0
iff
w=\pm w_{*}
.
On the other hand, we compute

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\text{Stat}}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w},f^{*})

\displaystyle=\#{\left\{r\geq 0:\lambda_{r}\geq\delta\right\}}\epsilon^{2}+\sum_{r\geq 0}(f^{*}_{r,w})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\lambda_{r}<\delta\right\}}

\displaystyle=\#{\left\{r\geq 0:\lambda_{r}\geq\delta\right\}}\epsilon^{2}+\sum_{r\geq 0}\rho^{2r}(g^{*}_{r})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\lambda_{r}<\delta\right\}}.

(65)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w},f^{*})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{w},f^{*})+\mathcal{E}_{\text{Stat}}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w},f^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geq 0}(1-\rho^{2r})(g^{*}_{r})^{2}+\#{\left\{r\geq 0:\lambda_{r}\geq\delta\right\}}\epsilon^{2}+\sum_{r\geq 0}\rho^{2r}(g^{*}_{r})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\lambda_{r}<\delta\right\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geq 0}\left[\epsilon^{2}+(1-\rho^{2r})(g^{*}_{r})^{2}\right]\bm{1}{\left\{\lambda_{r}\geq\delta\right\}}+\sum_{r\geq 0}(g^{*}_{r})^{2}\bm{1}{\left\{\lambda_{j}<\delta\right\}}.

(66)

Proposition D.1
.

Under Assumption
3
,
we have

\mathcal{E}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w},f^{*})-\mathcal{E}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w_{*}},f^{*})=\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{w},f^{*})\lesssim\begin{cases}1-\rho,&\alpha>1,\\
(1-\rho)\log(1-\rho)^{-1},&\alpha=1,\\
(1-\rho)^{\alpha},&\alpha\in(0,1).\end{cases}

(67)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, let us consider
\rho>0
.
Using elementary inequalities, we have
1-\rho^{2r}\leq 2r(1-\rho)
, so

\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{w},f^{*})=\sum_{r\geq 0}(1-\rho^{2r})(g^{*}_{r})^{2}\leq\sum_{r\geq 0}\min(1,2r(1-\rho))(g^{*}_{r})^{2}=2(1-\rho)\sum_{r\leq L}r(g^{*}_{r})^{2}+\sum_{r>L}(g^{*}_{r})^{2},

where
L=\frac{1}{2(1-\rho)}
.
Since
g^{*}_{r}\asymp r^{-\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}
, we have

\sum_{r>L}(g^{*}_{r})^{2}\lesssim L^{-\alpha}
.
In the meantime, we have

\sum_{r\leq L}r(g^{*}_{r})^{2}\lesssim\sum_{r\leq L}r^{-\alpha}\lesssim\begin{cases}1,&\alpha>1,\\
\log L,&\alpha=1,\\
L^{1-\alpha},&\alpha\in(0,1)\end{cases}

Combining the above inequalities, we conclude the result.
∎

D.1.1
Initialization

The following proposition shows the initialization of
\rho
.

Proposition D.2
.

Let
w\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
and
w_{*}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
be fixed.
Then, there is an absolute constant
c>0
depending on
d
such that

\mathbb{P}{\left\{\absolutevalue{\ang{w,w_*}}\geq\frac{t}{\sqrt{d}}\right\}}\geq 1-ct,\quad\forall t>0.

(68)

Moreover, we also have

\mathbb{P}{\left\{\absolutevalue{\ang{w,w_*}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\right\}}\geq 1-2\exp(-c d),\quad\forall d\geq 1.

(69)

Proof.

The proof of (
68
) is quite direct with the explicit density of
\left\langle{w,w_{*}}\right\rangle
.
See, for example, Lemma B.7 in
[
11
]
.
For (
69
), we can use a sub-Gaussian concentration for uniform distribution on the sphere.
∎

D.2
Population dynamics

In this subsection, let us consider the population dynamics of the adaptive kernel model for the single index model.
Let us denote by
\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left(f(x)-f^{*}(x)\right)^{2}
the population loss.
We consider the following equation, which is the population version of (
21
):

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\beta}_{r}&=-\nabla_{\beta_{r}}\mathcal{L},\quad\beta_{r}(0)=0,\quad r\geq 0,\\
\dot{w}&=-\nabla_{w}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathcal{L},\quad w(0)\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}).\end{aligned}\right.

(70)

Using (
55
), the population loss can be computed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[f^{*}(x)-f(x)\right]^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[g^{*}(\left\langle{w_{*},x}\right\rangle)-g(\left\langle{w,x}\right\rangle)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{r\geq 0}\left(g^{*}_{r}H_{r}(\left\langle{w_{*},x}\right\rangle)-g_{r}H_{r}(\left\langle{w,x}\right\rangle)\right)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r\geq 0}\left[(g^{*}_{r})^{2}+(g_{r})^{2}-2g^{*}_{r}g_{r}\rho^{r}\right].

Consequently, we find that

\nabla_{\beta_{r}}\mathcal{L}=-\lambda_{r}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\rho^{r}g^{*}_{r}-g_{r}),

(71)

so
\dot{\beta}_{r}=-\lambda_{r}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\rho^{r}g^{*}_{r}-g_{r})
.
Also, we have

\nabla_{w}\mathcal{L}=-\sum_{r\geq 1}rg^{*}_{r}g_{r}\rho^{r-1}w_{*}.

Taking the projection on the sphere, we find that

-\nabla_{w}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\mathcal{L}=-P_{w}^{\perp}\nabla_{w}\mathcal{L}=\left(\sum_{r\geq 1}rg^{*}_{r}g_{r}\rho^{r-1}\right)P_{w}^{\perp}w_{*}.

(72)

Let us further compute the dynamics of
\rho
.
We have

\dot{\rho}=\left\langle{\dot{w},w_{*}}\right\rangle=\left(\sum_{r\geq 1}rg^{*}_{r}g_{r}\rho^{r-1}\right)\left\langle{P_{w}^{\perp}w_{*},w_{*}}\right\rangle=\sum_{r\geq 1}rg^{*}_{r}g_{r}\rho^{r-1}(1-\rho^{2}),

(73)

where we notice that

\left\langle{P_{w}^{\perp}w_{*},w_{*}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{w_{*}-\left\langle{w,w_{*}}\right\rangle w,w_{*}}\right\rangle=1-\rho^{2}.

(74)

Let us collect the induced dynamics of
g_{r}=\lambda_{r}^{\frac{1}{2}}\beta_{r}
and
\rho
from (
70
) in the following

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{g}_{r}&=\lambda_{r}(\rho^{r}g^{*}_{r}-g_{r}),\quad r\geq 0,\\
\dot{\rho}&=\sum_{r\geq 1}rg^{*}_{r}g_{r}\rho^{r-1}(1-\rho^{2}).\end{aligned}\right.

(75)

The following proposition shows the basic properties of the population dynamics.

Proposition D.3
.

Consider the population dynamics (
70
).
Suppose
\rho(0)\neq 0
.
Then,

g_{r}^{*}g_{r}(t)\geq 0,\quad\forall r\geq 0,\quad\forall t\geq 0.

(76)

Also, for all
t\geq 0
,
\dot{\rho}(t)\geq 0
if
\rho(0)>0
, and
\dot{\rho}(t)\leq 0
if
\rho(0)<0
.

Proof.

It is an easy consequence of the dynamics (
75
).
We only illustrate the proof sketch, while a rigorous proof can be made by the standard ODE continuity argument.
The dynamics of
g_{r}
shows that
g_{r}(t)
will have the same sign as
\rho^{r}g^{*}_{r}
.
Hence, each term
\rho^{r-1}g_{r}^{*}g_{r}(t)=\rho^{-1}(\rho^{r}g^{*}_{r})g_{r}(t)
in
\dot{\rho}
will have the same sign as
\rho
.
Consequently,
\dot{\rho}
has the same sign as
\rho
and the result follows.
∎

Following Proposition
D.3
, we can assume that
g_{r}^{*}\geq 0
and consider
\rho(0)>0
without loss of generality in the subsequent analysis.

Proposition D.4
.

Consider the population dynamics (
70
).
Let Assumption
2
hold.
Suppose
\rho(0)=\rho_{0}\neq 0
.
Then,
\forall t\geq T^{\mathrm{app}}
,

\absolutevalue{\rho(t)}\geq\frac{1}{2},\quad\absolutevalue{g_{\rz}(t)}\geq 2^{-({r_{0}}+1)}\absolutevalue{g^*_{\rz}},

(77)

where

T^{\mathrm{app}}\lesssim\log\rho_{0}^{-1}+\rho_{0}^{-2({r_{0}}-1)}.

(78)

Proof.

We start with the first condition in (
77
).
Using Proposition
D.3
, it suffices to consider
\rho_{0}>0
and bound the first time when
\rho(t)\geq\frac{1}{2}
.
Moreover, since each term in
\dot{\rho}
is non-negative and
1-\rho^{2}\geq 3/4
when
\rho<1/2
, we have

\dot{\rho}\geq\frac{3{r_{0}}}{4}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\rho^{{r_{0}}-1}g_{{r_{0}}}=c{r_{0}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\rho^{{r_{0}}-1}g_{{r_{0}}}.

(79)

Also, we recall that

\dot{g}_{{r_{0}}}=\lambda_{{r_{0}}}(\rho^{{r_{0}}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}-g_{{r_{0}}}).

Let us take
L\coloneqq 1+\lceil\log_{2}\rho_{0}^{-1}\rceil
.
We define
\rho_{k}=2^{k}\rho_{0}
for
k<L
and
\rho_{L}=\frac{1}{2}
.
We also introduce the times

T_{0}^{\rho}=0,\quad T_{k}^{g}=\inf{\left\{t\geq T_{k}^{\rho}:g_{{r_{0}}}(t)\geq\frac{1}{2}\rho_{k}^{{r_{0}}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\right\}},\quad T_{k}^{\rho}=\inf{\left\{t\geq T_{k-1}^{g}:\rho(t)\geq\rho_{k}\right\}}.

(80)

Now, to bound
T_{k}^{g}
, we have

\dot{g}_{{r_{0}}}=\lambda_{{r_{0}}}(\rho^{{r_{0}}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}-g_{{r_{0}}})\geq\lambda_{{r_{0}}}\left(\rho_{k}^{{r_{0}}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}-g_{{r_{0}}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{{r_{0}}}\rho_{k}^{{r_{0}}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}},\quad t\in[T_{k}^{\rho},T_{k}^{g}],

so

T_{k}^{g}-T_{k}^{\rho}\leq\frac{\frac{1}{2}\rho_{k}^{{r_{0}}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}}{\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{{r_{0}}}\rho_{k}^{{r_{0}}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{{r_{0}}}}.

On the other hand, for
T_{k}^{\rho}
, we have

\dot{\rho}\geq\frac{{r_{0}}}{2}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\rho^{{r_{0}}-1}g_{{r_{0}}}\geq\frac{{r_{0}}}{2}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\cdot\rho_{k-1}^{{r_{0}}-1}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\rho_{k}^{{r_{0}}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}=\frac{{r_{0}}}{2^{{r_{0}}+1}}(g^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{2}\rho_{k}^{2{r_{0}}-1},\quad t\in[T_{k-1}^{g},T_{k}^{\rho}],

so

T_{k}^{\rho}-T_{k-1}^{g}\leq\frac{\rho_{k}-\rho_{k-1}}{\frac{{r_{0}}}{2^{{r_{0}}+1}}(g^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{2}\rho_{k}^{2{r_{0}}-1}}=\rho_{0}^{-2({r_{0}}-1)}2^{{r_{0}}}2^{-2({r_{0}}-1)k}{r_{0}}^{-1}(g^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{-2}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle T_{L}^{\rho}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\leq L}\left[(T_{k}^{\rho}-T_{k-1}^{g})+(T_{k-1}^{g}-T_{k-1}^{\rho})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\leq L}\left[\lambda_{{r_{0}}}^{-1}+\rho_{0}^{-2({r_{0}}-1)}2^{{r_{0}}}2^{-2({r_{0}}-1)k}{r_{0}}^{-1}(g^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{-2}\right]

\displaystyle=L\lambda_{{r_{0}}}^{-1}+\rho_{0}^{-2({r_{0}}-1)}2^{{r_{0}}}{r_{0}}^{-1}(g^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{-2}\sum_{k\leq L}2^{-2({r_{0}}-1)k}

\displaystyle\leq L\lambda_{{r_{0}}}^{-1}+\rho_{0}^{-2({r_{0}}-1)}2^{{r_{0}}}{r_{0}}^{-1}(g^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{-2}\left(1-2^{-2({r_{0}}-1)}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq L\lambda_{{r_{0}}}^{-1}+\rho_{0}^{-2({r_{0}}-1)}2^{{r_{0}}+1}{r_{0}}^{-1}(g^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{-2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda_{{r_{0}}}^{-1}\log\rho_{0}^{-1}+(g^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{-2}\rho_{0}^{-2({r_{0}}-1)},

and
T_{L}^{g}\leq T_{L}^{\rho}+\lambda_{{r_{0}}}^{-1}
.
∎

Proposition D.5
.

Consider the population dynamics (
70
).
Let Assumption
2
hold.
Suppose that (
77
) holds for some
t_{0}
.
Then,

1-\absolutevalue{\rho(t_0 + t)}\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp(-\rz 2^{-2\rz} (g^*_{\rz})^2 t).

(81)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\rho(t_{0})>0
and
g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}>0
.
By the monotonicity, we have

\dot{\rho}\geq{r_{0}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}\rho^{{r_{0}}-1}(1-\rho^{2})\geq{r_{0}}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}\rho^{{r_{0}}-1}(1-\rho)\geq{r_{0}}2^{-2{r_{0}}}(g^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{2}(1-\rho),

so the result follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

The monotonicity of the feature error measure follows from (
66
) and the monotonicity of
\absolutevalue{\rho}
in Proposition
D.3
.
For the initialization, from Proposition
D.2
we have

\frac{C}{\sqrt{d}}\leq\absolutevalue{\rho(0)}\leq\frac{1}{2}

with high probability.
Therefore, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{w(0)},f^{*})=\sum_{r\geq 0}(1-\rho(0)^{2r})(g^{*}_{r})^{2}\gtrsim\sum_{r\geq 1}(g^{*}_{r})^{2}\gtrsim 1.

On the other hand, since
\rho(0)\geq C/\sqrt{d}
, Proposition
D.4
and Proposition
D.5
shows that for some
t_{0}\asymp\log d+d^{{r_{0}}-1}
, we have

1-\absolutevalue{\rho(t_0 + t)}\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp(-C t).

Hence, the result follow from applying Proposition
D.1
and adjusting the constants.
∎

D.3
Sequence model

In this subsection, we consider the adaptive kernel dynamics in (
21
).

Using the symmetry of the dynamics with respect to negative
g_{r}^{*}
and negative
\rho(0)
in (
83
) and (
84
), in the subsequence parts,
we will assume that
g_{r}^{*}\geq 0
and
\rho(0)>0
without loss of generality.
Also, we will assume Assumption
2
holds without mentioning it explicitly.

D.3.1
Computing the Dynamics

Let us first compute the dynamics under the noisy sequence model.
We will combine the calculation in the population case and (
142
) to simplify the computation.

The
\beta_{r}
term

Recalling (
61
), we find that

\nabla_{\beta_{r}}f_{{\bm{m}}}=\lambda_{r}^{\frac{1}{2}}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}w^{{\bm{m}}}\delta_{r,\absolutevalue{\mm}}.

Let us define

e_{r}=e_{r}(w)=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}w^{{\bm{m}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{m}}}.

(82)

Then, combining with (
71
), (
142
) shows

-\nabla_{\theta_{r}}\hat{\mathcal{L}}=\lambda_{r}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\rho^{r}g_{r}^{*}-g_{r}+e_{r}\right),

and hence

\dot{g}_{r}=\lambda_{r}^{\frac{1}{2}}\dot{\theta}_{r}=\lambda_{r}\left(\rho^{r}g_{r}^{*}-g_{r}+e_{r}\right)

(83)

The
w
term

For the
w
term, we have

\nabla_{w}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}f_{\bm{m}}=g_{r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla_{w}^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}(w^{{\bm{m}}}).

Hence, using (
72
) and (
142
), we have

\dot{w}=\left(\sum_{r\geq 1}rg^{*}_{r}g_{r}\rho^{r-1}\right)P_{w}^{\perp}w_{*}+E,\quad E=\sum_{r\geq 0}g_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}_{w}(w^{{\bm{m}}})\varepsilon_{{\bm{m}}}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\dot{w},w_{*}}\right\rangle=\left[\sum_{r\geq 1}rg^{*}_{r}g_{r}\rho^{r-1}\right]\left\langle{P_{w}^{\perp}w_{*},w_{*}}\right\rangle+\left\langle{E,w_{*}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geq 1}rg^{*}_{r}g_{r}\rho^{r-1}(1-\rho^{2})+\tau,\quad\tau=\left\langle{E,w_{*}}\right\rangle.

(84)

D.3.2
Bounding the perturbation terms

Now, we will bound the perturbation terms
e_{r}
and
\tau
in (
83
) and (
84
) respectively by computing their covariance and using uniform bounds for Gaussian processes.
We note that
e_{r}
and
\tau
depend on the parameters
w
and
\bm{\beta}
, it is necessary for us to bound them uniformly over the parameter space.

Proposition D.6
.

Let
e_{r}
be defined in (
82
).
Then,
\mathrm{Cov}(e_{r}(u),e_{r}(v))=\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{r}/n
.
Hence, with probability at least
1-4\exp(-d)
, we have

\sup_{w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\absolutevalue{e_r(w)}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d\log r}{n}},\quad\forall r\geq 0.

(85)

Proof.

Let us first compute the covariance function.
Using the binomial theorem, we have

\mathrm{Cov}(e_{r}(u),e_{r}(v))=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}u^{{\bm{m}}}v^{{\bm{m}}}=\frac{1}{n}\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{r}

For the high probability bound, we apply Lemma
F.3
and notice that
u^{{\bm{m}}}
is Lipschitz in
u
with Lipschitz constant
\absolutevalue{\mm}
,
where we use a union bound on
r
so that the inequality holds for all
r
simultaneously.
∎

Proposition D.7
.

Let
\tau
be defined in (
84
).
Then, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-d)
,

\absolutevalue{\tau(w)}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}\sum_{r\geq 0}r^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{g_r},\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}.

(86)

Moreover, for any fixed
\nu\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-d)
,

\absolutevalue{\tau(w)}\lesssim\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{d\log\nu^{-1}}{n}}\sum_{r\geq 0}r^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{g_r},\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\backslash B_{\nu}(w_{*}).

(87)

Proof.

First, we can write

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=(w_{*})^{{\top}}\sum_{r\geq 0}g_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}_{w}(w^{{\bm{m}}})\varepsilon_{{\bm{m}}}

\displaystyle=(w_{*})^{{\top}}\sum_{r\geq 0}g_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}P_{w}^{\perp}\nabla_{w}(w^{{\bm{m}}})\varepsilon_{{\bm{m}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geq 0}g_{r}\tau_{r},

where

\tau_{r}=\tau_{r}(w)=(P_{w}^{\perp}w_{*})^{{\top}}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}_{w}(w^{{\bm{m}}})\varepsilon_{{\bm{m}}}.

To compute the covariance of
\tau_{r}
, let us define
a=P_{u}^{\perp}w_{*}
and
b=P_{v}^{\perp}w_{*}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\kappa(u,v)

\displaystyle\coloneqq n\mathrm{Cov}(\tau_{r}(u),\tau_{r}(v))=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i}a_{i}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla_{u_{i}}u^{{\bm{m}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{m}}}\right]\left[\sum_{j}b_{j}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\nabla_{v_{j}}v^{{\bm{m}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{m}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}b_{j}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}\nabla_{u_{i}}u^{{\bm{m}}}\nabla_{v_{j}}v^{{\bm{m}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}b_{j}\nabla_{u_{i}}\nabla_{v_{j}}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\binom{r}{{\bm{m}}}u^{{\bm{m}}}v^{{\bm{m}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}b_{j}\nabla_{u_{i}}\nabla_{v_{j}}\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}a_{i}b_{j}\left[r(r-1)\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{r-2}v_{i}u_{j}+r\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{r-1}\delta_{ij}\right]

\displaystyle=r(r-1)\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{r-2}\sum_{i,j}a_{i}v_{i}b_{j}u_{j}+r\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{r-1}\sum_{i}a_{i}b_{i}.

Rewriting it in vector form, we have

\displaystyle\kappa(u,v)=r(r-1)\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{r-2}\left\langle{P_{u}^{\perp}\eta^{*},v}\right\rangle\left\langle{P_{v}^{\perp}\eta^{*},u}\right\rangle+r\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle^{r-1}\left\langle{P_{u}^{\perp}\eta^{*},P_{v}^{\perp}\eta^{*}}\right\rangle

Introducing

\displaystyle\rho_{u}=\left\langle{u,\eta^{*}}\right\rangle,\quad\rho_{v}=\left\langle{v,\eta^{*}}\right\rangle,\quad q=\left\langle{u,v}\right\rangle,

and noticing that

\displaystyle P_{u}^{\perp}\eta^{*}=\eta^{*}-\rho_{u}u,\quad P_{v}^{\perp}\eta^{*}=\eta^{*}-\rho_{v}v,

we have

\displaystyle\kappa(u,v)=r(r-1)q^{r-2}(\rho_{v}-q\rho_{u})(\rho_{u}-q\rho_{v})+rq^{r-1}(1+q\rho_{u}\rho_{v}-\rho_{u}^{2}-\rho_{v}^{2})

When
u=v
, the first term vanishes as
\rho_{v}=\rho_{u}
and
q=1
,
so

\displaystyle\kappa(u,u)=r(1-\rho^{2}).

Now, let us bound the derivative of
\kappa(u,v)
.
We will frequently use the fact that

\norm{\nabla(fg)}=\norm{f \nabla g + g \nabla f}\leq\absolutevalue{f}\norm{\nabla g}+\absolutevalue{g}\norm{\nabla f}.

We have

\absolutevalue{q}\leq 1,\quad\absolutevalue{\rho_v - q \rho_u}\leq 2,\quad\absolutevalue{\rho_u - q \rho_v}\leq 2,\quad\absolutevalue{1 + q \rho_u \rho_v - \rho_u^2 - \rho_v^2}\leq 2.

and

\nabla_{u}q=v,\quad\nabla_{u}\rho_{u}=\eta^{*},\quad\nabla_{u}\rho_{v}=0,

so

\displaystyle\nabla_{u}(\rho_{v}-q\rho_{u})=-(\rho_{u}v+q\eta^{*}),\quad\nabla_{u}(\rho_{u}-q\rho_{v})=\eta_{*}-\rho_{v}v,

\displaystyle\nabla_{u}(1+q\rho_{u}\rho_{v}-\rho_{u}^{2}-\rho_{v}^{2})=\rho_{u}\rho_{v}v+q\rho_{v}\eta^{*}-2\rho_{u}\eta^{*}.

and

\norm{\nabla_u (\rho_v - q \rho_u) }\leq 2,\quad\norm{\nabla_u (\rho_u - q \rho_v) }\leq 2,\quad\norm{\nabla_u (1 + q \rho_u \rho_v - \rho_u^2 - \rho_v^2)}\leq 4.

Combining these, we have

\nabla_{u}\kappa_{r}(u,v)\leq C(r-1)(r-2),

where
C
is an absolute constant.
Consequently, we can apply Lemma
F.3
on
\sqrt{n}\tau_{r}
to obtain that with probability at least
1-Cr^{-2}\exp(-d)
, we have

\sup_{w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\absolutevalue{\tau_r(w)}\lesssim\sqrt{d(\log r)/n}.

Taking the summation, we have

\sup_{w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\absolutevalue{\tau(w)}\lesssim n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{r\geq 0}\sqrt{rd\log r}\absolutevalue{g_r}=\sqrt{d/n}\sum_{r\geq 0}r^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{g_r}.

Furthermore, let us introduce the scaled version

\bar{\tau}_{r}(w)=\sqrt{n}\left[r(1-\rho_{w}^{2})\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\tau_{r}(w),\quad\bar{\kappa}_{r}(u,v)=\mathrm{Cov}(\bar{\tau}_{r}(u),)\bar{\tau}_{r}(v))=\left[r^{2}(1-\rho_{u}^{2})(1-\rho_{v}^{2})\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}\kappa_{r}(u,v).

so that
\mathrm{Var}(\bar{\tau}_{r}(w))=1
.
We find

\nabla_{u}\bar{\kappa}_{r}(u,v)=\rho_{u}\eta^{*}(1-\rho_{u}^{2})^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-\rho_{v}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\kappa_{r}(u,v)+(1-\rho_{u}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-\rho_{v}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\nabla_{u}\kappa_{r}(u,v)

so

\norm{\nabla_u \bar{\kappa}_r(u,v) }\leq(1-\rho_{u}^{2})^{-1}+C(1-\rho_{u}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-\rho_{v}^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}(r-1)(r-2).

Consequently, if we have

1-\rho_{u}^{2}\geq\delta,\quad 1-\rho_{v}^{2}\geq\delta,

we have

\norm{\nabla_u \bar{\kappa}_r(u,v) }\leq C\delta^{-1}(r-1)(r-2).

Now we are ready to apply Lemma
F.3
on
\bar{\tau}_{r}
to obtain the high probability bound.
With probability at least
1-Cr^{-2}\exp(-d)
, we have

\sup_{w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\backslash B_{\delta}(w_{*})}\absolutevalue{\bar\tau_r(w)}\lesssim\sqrt{d(\log r+\log\delta^{-1})}.

Returning to
\tau_{r}
and taking the summation, we conclude the second bound.
∎

Proposition D.8
.

For any
r\geq 0
, we have

\absolutevalue{g_r(t)}\leq\left(\sup_{s\leq t}\absolutevalue{\rho(s)}^{r}\absolutevalue{g_r^*}+\absolutevalue{e_r}\right)\min(1,\lambda_{r}t).

(88)

Moreover, if
g^{*}_{r}\geq 0
and
\rho(s)\geq 0
for
s\in[0,t]
, then

g_{r}(t)\geq-\absolutevalue{e_r}\min(1,\lambda_{r}t).

(89)

Proof.

The first part follows from the dynamics in (
83
) and the comparison theorem.
For the second part, we simply notice

\lambda_{r}(\rho^{r}g_{r}^{*}-g_{r}+e_{r})\geq\lambda_{r}(-\absolutevalue{e_r}-g_{r})

and apply the comparison theorem.
∎

Corollary D.9
.

Let
(
85
)
hold and
n\gtrsim d
.
Then, we have

\absolutevalue{g_r(t)}\lesssim\left(\absolutevalue{g^*_r}+\sqrt{\frac{d\log r}{n}}\right)\min(1,\lambda_{r}t).

(90)

Consequently, the summation in Proposition
D.7
can be bounded by

\sum_{r\geq 0}r^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{g_r}\lesssim 1+(\log^{+}t)^{2}.

(91)

Proof.

It suffices to show the second part, where we can apply Proposition
F.6
.
∎

D.3.3
Training dynamics around initialization

In this subsection, we will consider the training dynamics around initialization.

Proposition D.10
.

Under Assumption
2
, assume
n\gtrsim d^{2{r_{0}}+s}
for some
s>0
.
Let (
85
) and (
86
) hold.
Let
\delta>0
be fixed.
Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, when
n,d
is large enough,
we have

\inf{\left\{t\geq 0:\absolutevalue{\rho(t)}\geq 1/2\right\}}=T^{\mathrm{app}}\lesssim\log d+d^{{r_{0}}-1},

(92)

where the constant in the
\lesssim
notation can depend on
\delta
.
Moreover, after a constant time,
\absolutevalue{\rho(t)}
is monotone increasing when
t\leq T^{\mathrm{app}}
.

Proof.

First, according to Proposition
D.2
,
we have
\absolutevalue{\rho(0)}\geq cd^{-1/2}
for some
c=c(\delta)>0
with probability at least
1-\delta
.
Also, by the symmetry of the dynamics, we consider
\rho(0)>0
without loss of generality.
In addition, we focus only on
\rho(t)\leq 1/2
and we will not mention it explicitly.
Taking
c_{0}=c/2
, we claim that we will have
\rho(t)\geq\rho_{0}\coloneqq c_{0}d^{-1/2}
for the range of
t
we are interested in.
We will prove this claim later.

Let us recall the dynamics of the component
{r_{0}}
:

\dot{g}_{{r_{0}}}=\lambda_{{r_{0}}}\left(\rho^{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}^{*}-g_{{r_{0}}}+e_{{r_{0}}}\right)

Thus, when
\rho(t)\geq c_{0}d^{-1/2}
, we have

\rho^{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}^{*}/2\geq Cc_{0}^{\frac{{r_{0}}}{2}}d^{-\frac{{r_{0}}}{2}}\geq C\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}\geq\absolutevalue{e_{\rz}},

when
n
is large enough since
n=\Omega(d^{{r_{0}}+1+s})
,
which implies that

\dot{g}_{{r_{0}}}\geq\lambda_{{r_{0}}}(\rho^{{r_{0}}}_{0}g_{{r_{0}}}^{*}/2-g_{{r_{0}}}).

(93)

Consequently,
g_{{r_{0}}}(t)
is monotone increasing and we have

T_{0}^{g}\coloneqq\inf{\left\{t\geq 0:g_{{r_{0}}}(t)\geq\frac{1}{4}\rho^{{r_{0}}}_{0}g_{{r_{0}}}^{*}\right\}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{{r_{0}}}}\lesssim 1.

Now we prove the claim for
t\leq T_{0}^{g}
.
We introduce

S_{0}={\left\{r\geq 1:(c_{0}d^{-1/2})^{r}\absolutevalue{g_r^*}\geq C\sqrt{d(\log r)/n}\geq\absolutevalue{e_r}\right\}}

and write

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geq 1}r\rho^{r-1}g^{*}_{r}g_{r}(1-\rho^{2})+\tau

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\in S_{0}}r\rho^{r-1}g^{*}_{r}g_{r}(1-\rho^{2})+\sum_{r\notin S_{0}}r\rho^{r-1}g^{*}_{r}g_{r}(1-\rho^{2})+\tau

\displaystyle=P_{0}+P_{1}+\tau.

For each
r\in S_{0}
, (
83
) shows that
g_{r}\geq 0
, so
P_{0}\geq 0
.
On the other hand, for
r\notin S_{0}
, Proposition
D.8
gives that

g_{r}(t)\geq-C\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)\absolutevalue{e_r}\geq-C\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)\sqrt{d(\log r)/n}.

Hence, using
\lambda_{r}\lesssim e^{-\gamma r}
and Proposition
F.6
, we have

\displaystyle P_{1}

\displaystyle\geq-C\sum_{r\notin S_{0}}r\rho^{r-1}g^{*}_{r}\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)\sqrt{d(\log r)/n}(1-\rho^{2})

\displaystyle\geq-C\sqrt{d/n}\sum_{r\notin S_{0}}r2^{-r}\sqrt{\log r}\absolutevalue{g^*_r}\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)

\displaystyle\geq-C\sqrt{d/n}.

Moreover, (
86
) and (
91
) give

\absolutevalue{\tau(t)}\lesssim\sqrt{d/n}(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2}).

Therefore, we have

\dot{\rho}(t)\geq-C\sqrt{d/n}(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2})\geq-C\sqrt{d/n},\quad\mbox{when}\quad t\leq T_{0}^{g},

and thus

\rho(T_{0}^{g})\geq\rho(0)-C\sqrt{d/n}T_{0}^{g}\geq c_{0}d^{-1/2}=\rho_{0},

since we have
n\gtrsim d^{2+s}
.

When
t\geq T_{0}^{g}
, we find that

P_{0}\geq{r_{0}}\rho^{{r_{0}}-1}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}(1-\rho^{2})\geq C\rho^{{r_{0}}-1}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\cdot\rho^{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}^{*}\gtrsim\rho_{0}^{2{r_{0}}-1}

Combining it with the bounds for
P_{1},\absolutevalue{\tau}
and using
n\gtrsim d^{2{r_{0}}+s}
,
as long as
t
is polynomial in
d,n
, we have

\rho_{0}^{2{r_{0}}-1}\gtrsim\sqrt{d/n}(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2})\gtrsim-P_{1}+\absolutevalue{\tau(t)},

and thus

\dot{\rho}(t)\geq P_{0}+P_{1}-\absolutevalue{\tau}\geq c\rho^{{r_{0}}-1}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}.

(94)

With (
93
) and (
94
), we can follow the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
D.4
.
We take
L\coloneqq 1+\lceil\log_{2}\rho_{0}^{-1}\rceil
define the times
T^{g}_{k}
and
T^{\rho}_{k}
similarly to (
80
) but replacing the constant
1/2
by
1/4
, and deduce that

T_{k}^{g}-T_{k}^{\rho}\leq\lambda_{{r_{0}}}^{-1}\lesssim 1,\quad T_{k}^{\rho}-T_{k-1}^{g}\lesssim\rho_{0}^{-2({r_{0}}-1)}2^{{r_{0}}}2^{-2({r_{0}}-1)k},

and

T_{L}^{\rho}\lesssim\log\rho_{0}^{-1}+\rho_{0}^{-2({r_{0}}-1)}\lesssim\log d+d^{{r_{0}}-1}.

The bound on
T_{L}^{\rho}
also shows that (
94
) is valid for the whole time interval
t\leq T_{L}^{\rho}
,
which implies the claim of
\rho(t)\geq\rho_{0}
as well.
∎

D.3.4
Training dynamics around convergence

Proposition D.11
.

Under Assumption
2
, assume
n\gtrsim d^{1+s}
for some
s>0
.
Let (
85
), (
86
) and (
87
) hold with
\nu=1/n
.
Suppose (
92
) holds for some time
t_{0}\lesssim\mathrm{poly}(d)
.
Then, there is some
t_{1}\leq t_{0}+C
such that

\absolutevalue{\rho(t_1 + s)}\text{ is monotone increasing and }1-\absolutevalue{\rho(t_1 + s)}\lesssim\exp(- c s),

provided that

1-\absolutevalue{\rho(t_1+s)}\gtrsim\frac{d\mathrm{polylog}(n,d)}{n}.

Proof.

We will first show that we have
g_{{r_{0}}}(t)\geq cg^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\geq c
,
\forall t\geq t_{1}=t_{0}+C
,
while we claim that
\rho(t)\geq 1/4
for
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
.
Recalling the dynamics of
g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}
, since
\rho(t)\geq 1/4
and
e_{{r_{0}}}
is bounded by (
85
), we have

\dot{g}_{{r_{0}}}=\lambda_{{r_{0}}}\left(\rho^{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}^{*}-g_{{r_{0}}}+e_{{r_{0}}}\right)\geq\lambda_{{r_{0}}}\left(cg_{{r_{0}}}^{*}-g_{{r_{0}}}\right),

so
g_{{r_{0}}}
is monotone increasing and we have
g_{{r_{0}}}(t_{1})\geq\frac{c}{2}g_{{r_{0}}}^{*}
for
t_{1}\leq t_{0}+\lambda_{{r_{0}}}^{-1}
.
Now let us prove the claim by lower bounding
\rho(t)
,
which shares similar argument as in the corresponding part in the proof of Proposition
D.10
.
We define

S_{0}^{\prime}={\left\{r\geq 1:4^{-r}\absolutevalue{g_r^*}\geq C\sqrt{d\log r/n}\geq\absolutevalue{e_r}\right\}},

and decompose

\dot{\rho}=\sum_{r\in S_{0}^{\prime}}r\rho^{r-1}g^{*}_{r}g_{r}(1-\rho^{2})+\sum_{r\notin S_{0}^{\prime}}r\rho^{r-1}g^{*}_{r}g_{r}(1-\rho^{2})+\tau=P_{0}^{\prime}+P_{1}^{\prime}+\tau.

It is easy to see that
P_{0}^{\prime}\geq 0
, while

\displaystyle P_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\geq-C\sum_{r\notin S_{0}}r\rho^{r-1}\absolutevalue{g^*_r}\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)\sqrt{d(\log r)/n}(1-\rho^{2})

\displaystyle\geq-C\sqrt{d/n}\sum_{r\notin S_{0}}r(\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{g^*_r}\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)

\displaystyle\geq-C\sqrt{d/n}\left(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2}\right),

Similarly, we use (
86
) and (
91
) to get
\absolutevalue{\tau(t)}\lesssim\sqrt{d/n}\left(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2}\right)
.
These show that

\dot{\rho}\geq-C\sqrt{d/n}\left(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2}\right)\geq-C\sqrt{d/n}(\log d)^{2},\quad\mbox{when}\quad t\in[t_{0},t_{1}],

and thus we prove the claim.

Now, let us show the convergence until
\rho(t)=1-d\mathrm{polylog}(n,d)/n
.
After
t\geq t_{1}
, we use (
87
) with
\nu=1/n
and (
91
) to get

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}

\displaystyle\geq{r_{0}}\rho^{{r_{0}}-1}g^{*}_{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}(1-\rho^{2})+P_{1}^{\prime}-\absolutevalue{\tau}

\displaystyle\geq c(1-\rho^{2})-C(1-\rho^{2})\sqrt{d/n}\left(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2}\right)-(1-\rho^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{d\log n}{n}}\left(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq c(1-\rho^{2})-(1-\rho^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{d\log n}{n}}\left(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2}\right).

Therefore, as long as
t
is polynomial in
n,d
and

1-\rho(t)^{2}\gtrsim\frac{d\log n}{n}\mathrm{polylog}(n,d),\quad\mbox{namely}\quad\rho(t)\leq 1-C\frac{d\mathrm{polylog}(n,d)}{n},

we have

\dot{\rho}\geq c(1-\rho^{2})\geq c(1-\rho),\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\rho(t_{1}+s)\geq 1-\frac{3}{4}\exp(-c s).

Consequently, it suffices to take an extra
\log n
time for
\rho(t)
to increase to
1-d\mathrm{polylog}(n,d)/n
.
The requirement on
t
is polynomial in
n,d
.
∎

D.3.5
Proof of Theorem
2.5

First, we can apply Proposition
D.6
and Proposition
D.7
with
\nu=1/n
that the estimates hold with probability at least
1-C\exp(-d)
.
Then, we can apply Proposition
D.10
for the initialization, taking
T_{1}=T^{\mathrm{app}}
,
and Proposition
D.11
for the convergence.
The monotonicity of
\mathcal{E}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w},f^{*})
follows from (
66
) and the monotonicity of
\rho
derived in the two propositions.
Here, we notice that if
\rho(t)
enters the monotone increasing phase in Proposition
D.10
, it will keep increasing until the convergence in Proposition
D.11
.
Finally, the bounds
\mathcal{E}^{*}(\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w(T_{1}+s)},f^{*})-\mathcal{E}^{*}(\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w_{*}},f^{*})
and
\mathcal{E}^{*}(\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w(T_{2})},f^{*})-\mathcal{E}^{*}(\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{w_{*}},f^{*})

come from the bound of
1-\absolutevalue{\rho(t)}
and Proposition
D.1
.

Appendix E
Proof for the multi-index model

Let us recall the multi-index model

f^{*}(x)=g^{*}(W_{*}^{\top}x),\quad W_{*}\in\mathrm{St}(d,p^{*}),

and the parameterization

\displaystyle\Phi_{W}(x)=\left(\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}H_{{\bm{m}}}(W^{\top}x)\right)_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}},\quad W\in\mathrm{St}(d,p),

\displaystyle f(x)=\left\langle{\bm{\beta},\Phi_{W}(x)}\right\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N}^{p})}=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\beta_{{\bm{m}}}\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}H_{{\bm{m}}}(W^{\top}x)=g(W^{\top}x),\quad g(u)=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\beta_{{\bm{m}}}H_{{\bm{m}}}(u)\in L^{2}(\gamma_{p}),

Regarding the weight sequence
\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}
, we recall that we take

\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}=\mu_{\absolutevalue{\mm}}\asymp\exp(-\gamma\abs{\mm})
for some fixed
\gamma>0
.

With auxiliary operators that will be introduced in Subsection
E.1.2
,
we can write
f^{*}=P_{W_{*}}g^{*}
and
f=P_{W}g
.
Moreover, under Assumption
4
, we will show in Subsection
E.1.6
that we can express

g_{2{\bm{r}}}=\nu_{{\bm{r}}}h_{r},\quad g^{*}_{2{\bm{r}}}=\nu_{{\bm{r}}}h_{r}^{*},\quad r=\absolutevalue{\rr},{\bm{r}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d},

while the other coefficients are zero,
where
\nu_{{\bm{r}}}
is a set of coefficients defined in Lemma
E.7
,
Consequently, we can suppose that the information index
m_{0}=2{r_{0}}
for some
{r_{0}}>0
.

Furthermore, let us denote
\varPsi=W^{\top}W_{*}
.
We consider the singular value decomposition
\varPsi=U\Sigma V^{\top}
.
We will also define some auxiliary quantities and we collect them here.

\begin{gathered}\phi_{r}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}},\qquad\phi_{0}=1,\quad\phi_{1}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sigma_{i}^{2}\\
\omega=-\frac{1}{K}\log(\Tr e^{-K\Sigma^2})=-\frac{1}{K}\log(\sum_{i=1}^p e^{-K \sigma_i^2})\leq\min(\sigma_{1}^{2},\ldots,\sigma_{p}^{2}),\end{gathered}

(95)

where
K
is a constant to be determined later.
We refer to (
112
) for the definition of
\phi_{r}
.

For convenience, we use
\left\langle{A,B}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}=\int A(x)B(x)\differential\gamma_{p}(x)
for compatible matrices (or vectors)
A,B
.
Also, we extend the definition of
P_{W},P_{W}^{{\top}},\mathcal{A}_{M}
(defined in Subsection
E.1.2
) to vector-valued functions by element-wise application.

E.1
Preliminaries

Let us first introduce some notations.
For a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
, we denote by
\Diag(A)
the diagonal matrix with the diagonal entries of
A
and
\Sym(A)=(A+A^{\top})/2
the symmetric part of
A
.
We note here that
\Diag(A)=\Diag(A^{\top})
and
\Diag(A\Lambda)=\Diag(A)\Lambda=\Lambda\Diag(A)
for a diagonal matrix
\Lambda
.

E.1.1
The Stiefel Manifold
{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)

The Stiefel manifold
{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
is the set of all real
(d\times p)
-matrices whose columns are orthonormal:

{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)={\left\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\mid X^{\top}X=I_{p}\right\}}.

It is well-known that
{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
is a Riemannian manifold with the metric induced by the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}
.
For each
X\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
, the tangent space
T_{X}{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
is given by

T_{X}{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)={\left\{Z\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\mid X^{\top}Z+Z^{\top}X=0\right\}}.

Moreover, we can compute the Riemannian gradient of a function restricted to
{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
using its Euclidean gradient.
Let
F:{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function.
Denote by
\nabla F(X)
the usual (Euclidean) gradient of
F
at
X

and by
\nabla^{{\mathcal{S}}}F(X)
the Riemannian gradient of
F
on
{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
.
Then,
\nabla^{{\mathcal{S}}}F(X)
is just the orthogonal projection of
\nabla F(X)
onto the tangent space
T_{X}{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
.
A convenient formula for this projection is given by

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\mathcal{S}}}F(X)={\Pi}_{T_{X}{\mathcal{S}}}(\nabla F(X)),\quad{\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}(Z)=Z-W\Sym(W^{\top}Z),

(96)

where
\Sym(A)=(A+A^{\top})/2
denotes the symmetric part of a matrix.

E.1.2
Auxiliary operators

Let us introduce some auxiliary operators that will be useful in the analysis of the multi-index model
[
10
]
.
For a matrix
W\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
, we define the operator
P_{W}:L^{2}(\gamma_{p})\to L^{2}(\gamma_{d})
by

f=P_{W}g,\quad f(x)=g(W^{\top}x).

(97)

Then, since
W^{\top}x\sim N(0,I_{p})
for
x\sim N(0,I_{d})
,
P_{W}
is isometric that

\norm{P_W g}_{\gamma_{d}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\gamma_{d}}g(W^{\top}x)^{2}=\mathbb{E}g(y)^{2}=\norm{g}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}.

Consequently, we can define its adjoint operator
P_{W}^{\top}:L^{2}(\gamma_{d})\to L^{2}(\gamma_{p})
by

\left\langle{P_{W}g,h}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\left\langle{g,P_{W}^{\top}h}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}.

Since
P_{W}
is isometric,
P_{W}^{\top}
is the orthogonal projection onto the space
L^{2}_{\Phi_{W}}={\left\{f=g(W^{\top}x),g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{p})\right\}}
.
In addition,
P_{W}^{{\top}}P_{W}=I_{L^{2}(\gamma_{p})}
and
P_{W}P_{W}^{{\top}}
is the orthogonal projection in
L^{2}(\gamma_{p})
onto the space
L^{2}_{\Phi_{W}}
.

Proposition E.1
.

The adjoint operator
P_{W}^{\top}:L^{2}(\gamma_{d})\to L^{2}(\gamma_{p})
is given explicitly by

(P_{W}^{\top}h)(y)=\mathbb{E}h(Wy+\xi),\quad\xi\sim N(0,I_{d}-WW^{\top}).

(98)

Proof.

Let us introduce
W^{\perp}
such that
\tilde{W}=(W,W_{\perp})
is an orthogonal matrix.
Then, letting
\tilde{y}=(y,y^{\prime})^{{\top}}=\tilde{W}^{\top}x
, we note that

\displaystyle\tilde{W}y=(W,W_{\perp})(y,y^{\prime})^{{\top}}=Wy+W_{\perp}y^{\prime},

so

\displaystyle\left\langle{P_{W}g,h}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\int g(W^{\top}x)h(x)\differential\gamma_{d}(x)=\int g(y)h(\tilde{W}y)\differential\gamma_{d}(y)=\int g(y)h(Wy+W_{\perp}y^{\prime})\differential\gamma_{d}(y)

\displaystyle=\int\int g(y)h(Wy+W_{\perp}y^{\prime})\differential\gamma_{p}(y)\differential\gamma_{d-p}(y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int g(y)\left[\int h(Wy+W_{\perp}y^{\prime})\differential\gamma_{d-p}(y^{\prime})\right]\differential\gamma_{p}(y)

Therefore, we conclude the following formula for
P_{W}^{\top}:L^{2}(\gamma_{d})\to L^{2}(\gamma_{p})
:

\displaystyle(P_{W}^{\top}h)(y)=\int h(Wy+W_{\perp}y^{\prime})\differential\gamma_{d-p}(y^{\prime}),

where
W_{\perp}
is a matrix such that
\tilde{W}=(W,W_{\perp})
is an orthogonal matrix.
