Alternatively, we can write

\displaystyle(P_{W}^{\top}h)(y)=\mathbb{E}h(Wy+\xi),\quad\xi\sim N(0,W_{\perp}W_{\perp}^{\top}).

Now,
W_{\perp}W_{\perp}^{\top}
is the orthogonal projection onto the orthogonal complement of the column space of
W
, so

\displaystyle W_{\perp}W_{\perp}^{\top}=I_{d}-WW^{\top}.

Finally, we conclude that

\displaystyle(P_{W}^{\top}h)(y)=\mathbb{E}h(Wy+\xi),\quad\xi\sim N(0,I_{d}-WW^{\top}).

(99)

∎

Moreover, for a matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}
with
\norm{M}\leq 1
, we define

\mathcal{A}_{M}:L^{2}(\gamma_{p_{2}})\to L^{2}(\gamma_{p_{1}}),\quad\mathcal{A}_{M}h(y)=\mathbb{E}h(M^{\top}y+\xi),\quad\xi\sim N(0,I_{p_{2}}-M^{\top}M).

(100)

The following proposition shows some basic properties of the operator
\mathcal{A}_{M}
and its relation to the operator
P_{W}
.

Proposition E.2
.

We have the following properties:

(i)

Let
W_{1}\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,p_{1})
and
W_{2}\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,p_{2})
.
Then,
P_{W_{1}}^{\top}P_{W_{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{W_{1}^{\top}W_{2}}
.

(ii)

\mathcal{A}_{M_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{M_{2}}=\mathcal{A}_{M_{1}M_{2}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{M}^{\top}=\mathcal{A}_{M^{\top}}
.

(iii)

If
Q
is an orthogonal matrix, then
P_{Q}^{\top}=P_{Q^{\top}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{Q}=P_{Q}
.

Proof.

To prove (i), we denote
\mathcal{A}_{W_{1},W_{2}}=P_{W_{1}}^{\top}P_{W_{2}}

and compute that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W_{1},W_{2}}h(y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(P_{W_{2}}h)(W_{1}y+\xi)=\mathbb{E}h(W_{2}^{\top}(W_{1}y+\xi))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}h(W_{2}^{\top}W_{1}y+W_{2}^{\top}\xi),

where
\xi\sim N(0,I_{d}-W_{1}W_{1}^{\top})
.
Now, denoting
M=W_{1}^{\top}W_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle W_{2}^{\top}\xi\sim N(0,W_{2}^{\top}(I_{d}-W_{1}W_{1}^{\top})W_{2})=N(0,I_{p_{2}}-M^{\top}M).

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{W_{1},W_{2}}h(y)=\mathcal{A}_{M}h(y)=\mathbb{E}h(M^{\top}y+\xi),\quad\xi\sim N(0,I_{p_{2}}-M^{\top}M).

For (ii), we note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{M_{1}M_{2}}h(y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}h((M_{1}M_{2})^{\top}y+\xi),\quad\xi\sim N(0,I_{p}-(M_{1}M_{2})^{\top}(M_{1}M_{2}))=N(0,I_{p}-M_{2}^{\top}M_{1}^{\top}M_{1}M_{2})

Now, we can take

\displaystyle\xi=M_{2}^{{\top}}\xi_{1}+\xi_{2},\quad\xi_{1}\sim N(0,I_{m}-M_{1}^{\top}M_{1}),\quad\xi_{2}\sim N(0,I_{p}-M_{2}^{\top}M_{2}),

we can check that the variance of
M_{2}^{{\top}}\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}

is
M_{2}^{{\top}}(I_{m}-M_{1}^{\top}M_{1})M_{2}+I_{p}-M_{2}^{\top}M_{2}=I_{p}-M_{2}^{\top}M_{1}^{\top}M_{1}M_{2}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{M_{1}M_{2}}h(y)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}h((M_{1}M_{2})^{\top}y+\xi)=\mathbb{E}h(M_{2}^{\top}M_{1}^{\top}y+M_{2}^{\top}\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}h(M_{2}^{\top}(M_{1}^{\top}y+\xi_{1})+\xi_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{M_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{M_{2}}h(y).

Furthermore, for the adjoint of
\mathcal{A}_{M}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{A}_{M}h,g}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p_{1}}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}h(M^{T}y+\xi_{2})g(y),\quad\xi_{2}\sim N(0,I_{p_{2}}-M^{\top}M),\quad y\sim N(0,I_{p_{1}})

Let us take

\displaystyle z=M^{{\top}}y+\xi_{2}\sim N(0,I_{p_{2}}),

we find that

\displaystyle\xi_{1}=y-Mz=(I_{p_{1}}-MM^{\top})y+M\xi_{2}\sim N(0,I_{p_{1}}-MM^{\top})

is independent of
z
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{A}_{M}h,g}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p_{1}}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}h(M^{T}y+\xi_{2})g(y)=\mathbb{E}h(z)g(Mz+\xi_{1})=\left\langle{h,\mathcal{A}_{M^{\top}}g}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p_{2}}}

The statement (iii) is straightforward from Proposition
E.1
and the definition of
\mathcal{A}
.
∎

Proposition E.3
.

Let
\Sigma
be a diagonal matrix with diagonal entries
\bm{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{p})
such that
\norm{\Sigma}\leq 1
.
Then,

\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}H_{{\bm{m}}}=\sigma^{{\bm{m}}}H_{{\bm{m}}}.

Proof.

From the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}
, we have

\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}H_{{\bm{m}}}(y)=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}H_{{\bm{m}}}(\Sigma y+\xi)=\prod_{j=1}^{p}\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{j}}H_{m_{j}}(\sigma_{j}y_{j}+\xi_{j}),\quad\xi\sim N(0,I_{p}-\Sigma^{2}).

Then, the result follows from using Lemma
C.4
for each
j
.
∎

First, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}(P_{W}g)(x)=\nabla_{x}g(W^{\top}x)=W(\nabla_{u}g)(W^{\top}x)=WP_{W}(\nabla_{u}g)

Lemma E.4
.

Let
W\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
,
g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{p})
and
h\in L^{2}(\gamma_{d})
.
Then,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}(P_{W}g)(x)

\displaystyle=P_{W}(W\nabla g),\quad\nabla_{x}(P_{W}^{{\top}}h)(x)=W^{\top}P_{W}^{{\top}}\nabla h,

(101)

\displaystyle\nabla_{W}P_{W}g

\displaystyle=xP_{W}(\nabla g)^{{\top}}

(102)

\displaystyle\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\left\langle{P_{W}g,h}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=-{\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}\int\nabla h(x)(P_{W}\nabla g)^{\top}\differential\gamma_{d}(x).

(103)

Proof.

To show (
101
), we compute

\nabla_{x}(P_{W}g)(x)=\nabla_{x}g(W^{\top}x)=W(\nabla g)(W^{\top}x)=WP_{W}(\nabla g)=P_{W}(W\nabla g).

Also, letting
\xi\sim N(0,I_{d}-WW^{\top})
, we have

\nabla_{x}(P_{W}^{{\top}}h)(x)=\nabla_{x}\mathbb{E}h(Wx+\xi)=\mathbb{E}W^{\top}(\nabla h)(Wx+\xi)=W^{\top}P_{W}^{{\top}}\nabla h.

For (
103
), we first compute

\displaystyle\nabla_{W}\left\langle{P_{W}g,h}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{W}\int g(W^{\top}x)h(x)\differential\gamma_{d}(x)=\int\nabla_{W}g(W^{\top}x)h(x)\differential\gamma_{d}(x)

\displaystyle=\int x(P_{W}(\nabla g)^{\top})h(x)\differential\gamma_{d}(x).

Using the Stein identity, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{W}\left\langle{P_{W}g,h}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=-\int\nabla_{x}\left[(P_{W}(\nabla g)^{\top})h(x)\right]\differential\gamma_{d}(x)

\displaystyle=-\int\left[h(x)\nabla_{x}(P_{W}(\nabla g)^{\top})+(\nabla h(x))P_{W}(\nabla g)^{\top}\right]\differential\gamma_{d}(x)

For the first part, we find that

\int h(x)\nabla_{x}(P_{W}(\nabla g)^{\top})\differential\gamma_{d}(x)=\int h(x)WP_{W}(\nabla^{2}g)\differential\gamma_{d}(x)=W\int P_{W}(\nabla^{2}g)h(x)\differential\gamma_{d}(x).

Since
\int P_{W}(\nabla^{2}g)h(x)\differential\gamma_{d}(x)
is a symmetric matrix, we find that

{\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}\int h(x)\nabla_{x}(P_{W}(\nabla g)^{\top})\differential\gamma_{d}(x)=0.

Therefore, we only have the second part in our final result.
∎

Lemma E.5
.

For
M\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{1}\times p_{2}}
with
\norm{M}\leq 1
and
f,f_{i}\in L^{2}(\gamma_{p_{1}})
,
i=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}(\mathcal{A}_{M}f)(x)=\mathcal{A}_{M}(M\nabla f)

(104)

Proof.

The proof of (
104
) is similar to the proof of (
101
) in Lemma
E.4
,
where we recall that
\mathcal{A}_{M}f(x)=\mathbb{E}f(M^{\top}x+\xi)
,
\xi\sim N(0,I_{p_{2}}-M^{\top}M)
.
∎

E.1.3
Derivatives and Singular Value Decomposition

Let us be given a flow of matrix
X(t)
, we take its singular value decomposition (SVD)
X(t)=U(t)\Sigma(t)V(t)^{\top}
, where
U(t),V(t)
are orthogonal matrices and
\Sigma(t)
is a diagonal matrix.
Then we have

\dot{X}=\dot{U}\Sigma V^{\top}+U\dot{\Sigma}V^{\top}+U\Sigma\dot{V}^{\top}.

Since
U,V
are orthogonal, we have
\dot{U}^{\top}U+U^{\top}\dot{U}=0,\quad\dot{V}^{\top}V+V^{\top}\dot{V}=0.

To show the dynamics of
\Sigma
, we have

\dot{\Sigma}=U^{{\top}}\dot{X}V-\left(U^{{\top}}\dot{U}\Sigma+\Sigma\dot{V}^{\top}V\right)=\Diag(U^{{\top}}\dot{X}V)-\Diag\left(U^{{\top}}\dot{U}\Sigma+\Sigma\dot{V}^{\top}V\right)

Since
U^{{\top}}\dot{U}
and
\dot{V}^{\top}V
are skew-symmetric and
\Sigma
is diagonal, we have

\Diag(U^{{\top}}\dot{U}\Sigma)=\Diag(\Sigma\dot{V}^{\top}V)=0.

Therefore,

\dot{\Sigma}=\Diag(U^{{\top}}\dot{X}V).

(105)

Consequently, for the directional derivative, we also have

D_{H}\Sigma=\Diag(U^{{\top}}(D_{H}X)V),\quad D_{H}\sigma_{j}=u_{j}^{{\top}}(D_{H}X)v_{j},

(106)

where
u_{j},v_{j}
are the
j
-th columns of
U,V
respectively.

E.1.4
Matrix calculus

Let
M
be a symmetric matrix and
f
be a function.
Then, we can define
f(M)
via the spectral decomposition
M=Q\Lambda Q^{\top}
that

f(M)=Qf(\Lambda)Q^{\top}
, where
\Lambda
is a diagonal matrix with the eigenvalues of
M
and
f(\Lambda)
is applied on the diagonal entry-wise.
Let
X
be a general matrix, we can also define
f(X^{\top}X)
,
f(XX^{\top})
.
Suppose
X=U\Sigma V^{\top}
is the SVD of
X
.
Then, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle f(X^{\top}X)=Vf(\Sigma^{2})V^{\top},\qquad f(XX^{\top})=Uf(\Sigma^{2})U^{\top},

(107)

\displaystyle Xf(X^{\top}X)=f(XX^{\top})X=Uf(\Sigma^{2})\Sigma V^{{\top}}\qquad X^{{\top}}f(XX^{\top})=f(X^{\top}X)X^{{\top}}=V\Sigma f(\Sigma^{2})U^{\top},

E.1.5
Initialization

The following proposition shows the behavior of the singular values of a random initialization on the Stiefel manifold
[
3
]
.
It is adapted from Lemma 3.14 in
[
10
]

Proposition E.6
.

Let
W\sim\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{S}}(d,p))
and
W_{*}\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
be fixed.
Then, for any
\delta>0
, there are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending on
\delta,p
such that

\mathbb{P}{\left\{\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{d}}\leq\sigma_{p}\leq\sigma_{1}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}\right\}}\geq 1-\delta.

(108)

E.1.6
Rotationally Invariant Functions

Let us consider a rotationally invariant function
g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{p})
whose expansion is given by

g=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}g_{{\bm{m}}}H_{{\bm{m}}}
.
We will give a more explicit form of the gradient of
g
.

Lemma E.7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}(p)
be the subspace of rotationally invariant functions in
L^{2}(\gamma_{p})
.
Then, we have

\mathcal{G}(p)={\left\{g=\sum_{r\geq 0}h_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}H_{2{\bm{r}}}:\sum_{r\geq 0}h_{r}^{2}<\infty\right\}},

(109)

where the coefficients
\nu_{{\bm{r}}}
are given by

\nu_{{\bm{r}}}\coloneqq C_{r}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\binom{2{\bm{r}}}{{\bm{r}}}^{1/2}=C_{r}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{(2{\bm{r}})!}}{({\bm{r}})!},\quad C_{r}=4^{r}\frac{(p/2)_{r}}{{r!}},~r=\absolutevalue{\rr},

(110)

satisfying
\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}=1
.
Here,
(a)_{r}=a(a+1)\cdots(a+r-1)
is the rising factorial.

Proof.

First, since
g
is rotationally invariant,
g
must be even in each variable,
so we must have
g_{{\bm{m}}}=0
if
{\bm{m}}
has an odd entry.
It remains to consider those
{\bm{m}}=2{\bm{r}}
.
Fix
{\bm{r}}
and let
r=\absolutevalue{\rr}
.
For any orthogonal matrix
Q
, let us compute the Hermite coefficients of
P_{Q}g
.
Using Lemma
C.1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle{P_{Q}g,H_{2{\bm{r}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\sum_{\bm{s}}g_{2\bm{s}}H_{2\bm{s}},P_{Q}^{\top}H_{2{\bm{r}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}g_{2\bm{s}}\left\langle{H_{2\bm{s}},P_{Q}^{\top}H_{2{\bm{r}}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}g_{2\bm{s}}\sqrt{(2\bm{s})!(2{\bm{r}})!}[\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}\beta^{2\bm{s}}]\exp(\alpha^\T Q^\T\beta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2{\bm{r}}!}[\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}]\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}g_{2\bm{s}}\sqrt{(2\bm{s})!}[\beta^{2\bm{s}}]\exp((Q \alpha)^\T\beta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2{\bm{r}}!}[\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}]\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}(Q\alpha)^{2\bm{s}}((2\bm{s})!)^{-\frac{1}{2}}g_{2\bm{s}}

On one hand, let us suppose that
g
is of the form
g=\sum_{r\geq 0}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}h_{r}H_{2{\bm{r}}}
.
We denote
v=Q\alpha
.
Plugging in the expression of
g_{2\bm{s}}
yields

\displaystyle\left\langle{P_{Q}g,H_{2{\bm{r}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=C_{\absolutevalue{r}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(2\bm{r})!}[\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}]\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}\frac{1}{\bm{s}!}h_{r}v^{2\bm{s}}

\displaystyle=C_{\absolutevalue{r}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(2\bm{r})!}h_{r}[\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}]\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}\frac{1}{\bm{s}!}\prod_{i}(v_{i}^{2})^{s_{i}}

\displaystyle=C_{\absolutevalue{r}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(2\bm{r})!}h_{r}[\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}]\frac{1}{r!}(\sum_{i}v_{i}^{2})^{r}

\displaystyle=C_{\absolutevalue{r}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(2\bm{r})!}h_{r}[\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}]\frac{1}{r!}\norm{Q \alpha}^{2r}

\displaystyle=C_{\absolutevalue{r}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(2\bm{r})!}h_{r}[\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}]\frac{1}{r!}\norm{\alpha}^{2r}

\displaystyle=C_{\absolutevalue{r}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{(2{\bm{r}})!}}{({\bm{r}})!}h_{r},

so
P_{Q}g
and
g
have the same coefficients, showing that
P_{Q}g=g
and thus
g\in\mathcal{G}(p)
.

On the other hand, let us suppose that
P_{Q}g=g
for all orthogonal matrices
Q
.
Let us define the polynomial

p(\alpha)=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}g_{2\bm{s}}\frac{\alpha^{2\bm{s}}}{\sqrt{(2\bm{s})!}},

which is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
2r
with even exponents.
Then, for any orthogonal matrix
Q
, we have

g_{2{\bm{r}}}=\left\langle{P_{Q}g,H_{2{\bm{r}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}=\sqrt{(2{\bm{r}})!}[\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}]p(Q\alpha),\quad\quad\mbox{for}\quad{\bm{r}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p},

which shows that
p(Q^{\top}\alpha)
has the same coefficients of
\alpha^{2{\bm{r}}}
as
p(\alpha)
.
Since the set
{\left\{\alpha^{2\bm{s}}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r\right\}}
spans the space of even homogeneous polynomials of degree
2r
,
this equation implies that
p(Q^{\top}\alpha)
is the same for all
Q\in O(p)
.
For degree
2r
, such rotationally invariant polynomials are known to be multiples of
\norm{\alpha}^{2r}
.
Hence, using the multinomial theorem, we get

p(\alpha)=c_{r}\norm{\alpha}^{2r}=c_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}\frac{r!}{\bm{s}!}\alpha^{2\bm{s}}.

Equating coefficients gives

g_{2\bm{s}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\bm{s})!}}=c_{r}\frac{r!}{\bm{s}!}\implies g_{2\bm{s}}=c_{r}r!\frac{\sqrt{(2\bm{s})!}}{\bm{s}!}.

Therefore,
g
must be the form in (
109
).

Finally, the normalizing constant
C_{r}
is computed by Proposition
E.8
with
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{1}
.
∎

Proposition E.8
.

Let
p,r\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}_{+}
.
Then

f(\bm{\lambda})=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\binom{2{\bm{r}}}{{\bm{r}}}\bm{\lambda}^{{\bm{r}}}=[z^{r}]\prod_{j=1}^{p}(1-4\lambda_{j}z)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(111)

Particularly, if
\bm{\lambda}=\bm{1}
, we have

f(\bm{1})=[z^{r}](1-4z)^{-p/2}=4^{r}\frac{(p/2)_{r}}{r!}.

Proof.

Recall the identity

(1-4\alpha)^{-1/2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{2n}{n}\alpha^{n}.

Taking
\alpha=\lambda_{j}z_{j}
, we have

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{p}(1-4\lambda_{j}z)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{r_{j}=0}^{\infty}\binom{2r_{j}}{r_{j}}\lambda_{j}^{r_{j}}z^{r_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1},\dots,n_{p}}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\binom{2r_{j}}{r_{j}}\lambda_{j}^{r_{j}}z^{r_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{r}}}\frac{(2{\bm{r}})!}{({\bm{r}}!)^{2}}\lambda^{{\bm{r}}}z^{\absolutevalue{\rr}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geq 0}\left[\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\frac{(2{\bm{r}})!}{({\bm{r}}!)^{2}}\lambda^{{\bm{r}}}\right]z^{r}.

The proposition follows by comparing the coefficients.
∎

Let
\varPsi\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
with
\norm{\Rho}\leq 1
.
Let
\varPsi=U\Sigma V^{{\top}}
be the SVD of
\varPsi
and
\sigma
be the diagonal of
\Sigma
.
Then, using Proposition
E.8
, we can introduce the function

\phi_{r}(\varPsi)\coloneqq\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}=C_{r}^{-1}[z^{r}]\det(I - 4 \Rho^T \Rho z)^{-\frac{1}{2}},\qquad C_{r}=4^{r}\frac{(p/2)_{r}}{r!},

(112)

where we define
\phi_{0}=1
.
This function is well-defined and depends only on the singular values of
\varPsi
.
To see this, we use Proposition
E.8
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}

\displaystyle=C_{r}^{-1}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\binom{2{\bm{r}}}{{\bm{r}}}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}

\displaystyle=C_{r}^{-1}[z^{r}]\prod_{j=1}^{p}(1-4\sigma_{j}^{2}z)^{-\frac{1}{2}}=[z^{r}]\det(I - 4 \Sigma^2 z)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=C_{r}^{-1}[z^{r}]\det(I - 4 \Rho^T \Rho z)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Moreover, we notice that
\phi_{1}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sigma_{j}^{2}.

Corollary E.9
.

Consider a loss
L(g)
on the space of rotationally invariant functions.
Let
g=\sum_{{\bm{m}}}g_{{\bm{m}}}H_{{\bm{m}}}
.
Denote by
\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}
the classical gradient with respect to the coefficient
g_{{\bm{m}}}

and by
\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}
the gradient
\mathcal{G}(p)
.
Then, we have

\nabla_{g_{2{\bm{r}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}L(g)=\nu_{{\bm{r}}}\nabla_{h_{r}}L(g),\quad\nabla_{h_{r}}L(g)=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}\nabla_{g_{2{\bm{r}}}}L(g),\quad r=\absolutevalue{\rr}.

(113)

Regarding the coefficient, we also have the following properties.

Proposition E.10
.

Let the coefficients
\nu_{{\bm{r}}}
be defined as in Lemma
E.7
.
Then, we have

\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{k}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}=\frac{r}{p},\quad\forall k=1,\ldots,p.

(114)

Moreover, defining

A_{ij}=\begin{cases}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}(2r_{i})(2r_{j})\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2},&i\neq j\\
\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}(2r_{i})(2r_{i}-1)\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2},&i=j\end{cases}

we have

\sum_{i=1}^{p}A_{ki}=\frac{2r(2r-1)}{p},\quad\forall k=1,\ldots,p.

(115)

Proof.

Let
A_{j}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{j}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}
.
Then, by symmetry, we have
A_{1}=\dots=A_{p}
, so

A_{j}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}A_{j}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}(r_{1}+\dots+r_{p})=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}r=\frac{r}{p}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}=\frac{r}{p}.

For the statement regarding
A_{ij}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p}A_{ki}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}(2r_{k})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{p}(2r_{i})-1\right)\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}A_{ki}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}(2r_{k})\left(2r-1\right)\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}=(2r-1)\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}2r_{k}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2r(2r-1)}{p}.

∎

Proposition E.11
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{G}(p)
be a rotationally invariant function.
Then, for any orthogonal matrix
Q
, we have

\nabla g=QP_{Q}\nabla g=P_{Q}Q\nabla g,\qquad\nabla^{2}g=Q(P_{Q}\nabla^{2}g)Q^{\top}.

Proof.

Since
g
is rotationally invariant, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}g(x)=\nabla_{x}(P_{Q}g)(x)=P_{Q}(Q\nabla g)=QP_{Q}(\nabla g).

Taking the second derivative, we have

\nabla^{2}_{x}g(x)=\nabla_{x}^{\top}[QP_{Q}(\nabla g)]=Q\nabla_{x}^{\top}(P_{Q}(\nabla g))=Q(P_{Q}\nabla^{2}g)Q^{\top}.

∎

Corollary E.12
.

Let
\bar{H}_{r}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}H_{2{\bm{r}}}
be the projection of
H_{r}
onto
\mathcal{G}(p)
.
Then, we have

\int\nabla\bar{H}_{r}(\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{{\top}}\differential\gamma_{p}=\frac{2r}{p}I_{p}.

\int(\nabla^{2}\bar{H}_{r})^{2}\differential\gamma_{p}=c_{r}I_{p},\quad c_{r}=\frac{2r(2r-1)}{p}\leq 4r^{2}.

Proof.

Using Proposition
E.11
, we find that

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\int\nabla\bar{H}_{r}(\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{{\top}}\differential\gamma_{p}=\int Q(P_{Q}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})(P_{Q}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{{\top}}Q^{{\top}}\differential\gamma_{p}

\displaystyle=Q\int(P_{Q}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})(P_{Q}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{{\top}}\differential\gamma_{p}Q^{{\top}}=Q\int\nabla\bar{H}_{r}(\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{{\top}}\differential\gamma_{p}Q^{{\top}}

\displaystyle=QAQ^{\top},

so
A
is a scalar multiple of the identity.

Using the derivative of the Hermite polynomial, we further compute that

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{1}}\bar{H}_{r}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}\sqrt{2r_{1}}H_{2{\bm{r}}-2e_{1}},

so

\displaystyle A_{11}=\int(\nabla_{x_{1}}\bar{H}_{r})^{2}\differential\gamma_{p}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}2r_{1}=\frac{2r}{p}.

∎

E.2
The Feature Error Measure

Let us recall that the subspace

L^{2}_{\Phi_{W}}=\overline{\spn}{\left\{H_{{\bm{m}}}(W^{\top}x),~{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}\right\}}={\left\{f=g(W^{\top}x),~g\in L^{2}(\gamma_{p})\right\}}.

Then, as
P_{W}^{\top}
is the projection onto
L^{2}_{\Phi_{W}}
, the orthogonal projection of
f^{*}
onto
L^{2}_{\Phi_{W}}
is given by

f^{*}_{W}\coloneqq(P_{W}P_{W}^{\top})f^{*}=P_{W}P_{W}^{\top}P_{W_{*}}g^{*}=P_{W}\mathcal{A}_{\varPsi}g^{*}=P_{W}\mathcal{A}_{U}\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}_{V^{\top}}g^{*}=P_{W}P_{U}\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}g^{*},

where we use the SVD of
\varPsi=U\Sigma V^{\top}
, Proposition
E.2
and the rotation invariance of
g^{*}
.
Consequently, using Proposition
E.3
, we have

\norm{f^*_W}_{\gamma_{d}}^{2}=\norm{P_W P_U \caA_{\Sigma} g^*}_{\gamma_{d}}^{2}=\norm{\caA_{\Sigma} g^*}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\sigma^{2{\bm{m}}}(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}.

Then, the projection error is

\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{W},f^{*})=\norm{f^* - f^*_W }_{\gamma_{d}}^{2}=\norm{f^*}_{\gamma_{d}}^{2}-\norm{f^*_W}_{\gamma_{d}}^{2}=\norm{g^*}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}-\norm{f^*_W}_{\gamma_{d}}^{2}=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}(1-\sigma^{2{\bm{m}}})(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}.

To compute the statistical error, recalling the definition, let us introduce

g^{*}_{W,{\bm{m}}}\coloneqq\left\langle{f^{*},H_{{\bm{m}}}(W^{\top}x)}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\left\langle{f^{*},P_{W}H_{{\bm{m}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\left\langle{g^{*}_{W},H_{{\bm{m}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}},

where

g^{*}_{W}\coloneqq P_{W}^{\top}f^{*}=P_{W}^{\top}P_{W_{*}}g^{*}=\mathcal{A}_{\varPsi}g^{*}=\mathcal{A}_{U}\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}_{V^{\top}}g^{*}=P_{U}\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}g^{*}.

Then, since
\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}=\mu_{\absolutevalue{\mm}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\bm{1}{\left\{\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}<\delta\right\}}(g^{*}_{W,{\bm{m}}})^{2}=\sum_{r\geq 0}\bm{1}{\left\{\mu_{r}<\delta\right\}}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}(g^{*}_{W,{\bm{m}}})^{2}.

Moreover, let us consider the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{r}=\spn{\left\{H_{{\bm{m}}},~\absolutevalue{\mm}=r\right\}}
, and let
P_{\mathcal{H}_{r}}
the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{r}
.
Since
\mathcal{H}_{r}
and
\mathcal{H}_{r}^{\perp}
are both invariant under
P_{U}
from Corollary
C.3
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}(g^{*}_{W,{\bm{m}}})^{2}=\norm{P_{\caH_r} g^*_{W}}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}=\norm{P_{\caH_r} P_U \caA_{\Sigma} g^*}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}=\norm{ P_U P_{\caH_r} \caA_{\Sigma} g^*}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}=\norm{P_{\caH_r} \caA_{\Sigma} g^*}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\sigma^{2{\bm{m}}}(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}.

Consequently,

\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}=\sum_{r\geq 0}\bm{1}{\left\{\mu_{r}<\delta\right\}}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\sigma^{2{\bm{m}}}(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}.

On the other hand,

\mathcal{E}_{\text{V}}=\#{\left\{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}:\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}\geq\delta\right\}}\epsilon^{2}=\epsilon^{2}\sum_{r\geq 0}\bm{1}{\left\{\mu_{r}\geq\delta\right\}}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}1=\epsilon^{2}\sum_{r\geq 0}\bm{1}{\left\{\mu_{r}\geq\delta\right\}}\binom{p+r-1}{r}.

Merge the two terms, we have the following proposition.

Proposition E.13
.

Under Assumption
4
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{W},f^{*})=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}(1-\sigma^{2{\bm{m}}})(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2},

(116)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\text{B}}=\sum_{r\geq 0}\bm{1}{\left\{\mu_{r}<\delta\right\}}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\sigma^{2{\bm{m}}}(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2},\quad\mathcal{E}_{\text{V}}=\epsilon^{2}\sum_{r\geq 0}\bm{1}{\left\{\mu_{r}\geq\delta\right\}}\binom{p+r-1}{r}.

(117)

Proposition E.14
.

Under Assumption
6
,
we have

\mathcal{E}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{W},f^{*})-\mathcal{E}(\delta,\epsilon^{2};\Phi_{W_{*}},f^{*})=\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{W},f^{*})\lesssim\begin{cases}1-\rho,&\alpha>1,\\
(1-\rho)\log(1-\rho)^{-1},&\alpha=1,\\
(1-\rho)^{\alpha},&\alpha\in(0,1).\end{cases}

(118)

where
\rho=\min_{j}\sigma_{j}^{2}
.

Proof.

The proof resembles that of Proposition
D.1
, but we deal with multi-index now.
With (
116
), taking
L=c(1-\rho)^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{w},f^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}(1-\sigma^{2{\bm{m}}})(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}\leq\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}(1-\rho^{\absolutevalue{\mm}})(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}\leq\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\min(1,\absolutevalue{\mm}(1-\rho))(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}\leq L}2\absolutevalue{\mm}(1-\rho)(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}+\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}>L}(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}

We will use the fact that

\#{\left\{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}:\absolutevalue{\mm}=r\right\}}=\binom{p+r-1}{r}\lesssim r^{p-1}.

Also, recall that
\absolutevalue{g^*_{\mm}}\lesssim\absolutevalue{\mm}^{-\frac{\alpha+p}{2}}
.

The first term is bounded by

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2(1-\rho)\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}\leq L}\absolutevalue{\mm}^{-(\alpha+p-1)}=2(1-\rho)\sum_{r\leq L}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=r}\absolutevalue{\mm}^{-(\alpha+p-1)}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2(1-\rho)\sum_{r\leq L}r^{p-1}r^{-(\alpha+p-1)}=2(1-\rho)\sum_{r\leq L}r^{-\alpha}.

Therefore,

I_{1}\lesssim\begin{cases}1-\rho,&\alpha>1,\\
(1-\rho)\log(1-\rho)^{-1},&\alpha=1,\\
(1-\rho)^{\alpha},&\alpha\in(0,1).\end{cases}

For the second term, we have similarly

I_{2}\lesssim\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}>L}\absolutevalue{\mm}^{-(\alpha+p)}\lesssim\sum_{r>L}r^{p-1}r^{-(\alpha+p)}=\sum_{r>L}r^{-(\alpha+1)}\lesssim L^{-\alpha}.

Combining the bounds, we conclude the proposition.
∎

E.3
Population Dynamics

Let us introduce the population dynamics of the multi-index model.
Let us denote by
\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\norm{f - f^*}^{2}_{L^{2}(\gamma_{d})}
the population loss.
Following (
28
), we consider

\left\{\begin{aligned} \dot{\bm{\beta}}(t)&=-\nabla_{\bm{\beta}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}\mathcal{L},\quad\bm{\beta}(0)=\bm{0},\\
\dot{W}(t)&=-\nabla_{W}^{\mathrm{St}(d,p)}\mathcal{L},\quad W(0)\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathrm{St}(d,p)).\end{aligned}\right.

(119)

E.3.1
Computing the Gradient Flow

Using the auxiliary operators and noticing that
f=P_{W}g
and
f^{*}=P_{W_{*}}g^{*}
, we can write the population loss as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\norm{f - f^*}^{2}_{L^{2}(\gamma_{d})}=\frac{1}{2}\norm{f}_{L^{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\norm{f^*}_{L^{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}-\left\langle{f,f^{*}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\gamma_{d})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\norm{g}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\norm{g^*}_{\gamma_{p^{*}}}^{2}-\left\langle{P_{W}g,P_{W_{*}}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}=\frac{1}{2}\norm{g}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\norm{g^*}_{\gamma_{p^{*}}}^{2}-\left\langle{g,\mathcal{A}_{W^{\top}W_{*}}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}},

where we use the fact that
P_{W}
is isometric.
Moreover, using Proposition
E.2
and the rotation invariance of
g
and
g^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle{g,\mathcal{A}_{W^{\top}W_{*}}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{g,\mathcal{A}_{\varPsi}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}=\left\langle{g,\mathcal{A}_{U}\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}_{V^{\top}}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\mathcal{A}_{U^{\top}}g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}_{V^{\top}}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}=\left\langle{P_{U^{\top}}g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}P_{V^{\top}}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

Consequently, we obtain

\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\norm{g}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\norm{g^*}_{\gamma_{p^{*}}}^{2}-\left\langle{g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}.

(120)

The dynamics of
h_{r}
.

Using (
120
), we have

\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}\mathcal{L}=\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}\frac{1}{2}\norm{g}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}-\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}\left\langle{g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}=g_{{\bm{m}}}-\left(\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}g^{*}\right)_{{\bm{m}}},

Using Corollary
E.9
, we find that for
{\bm{m}}=2{\bm{r}}
,
\absolutevalue{\rr}=r
, we have

\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}\mathcal{L}=\nu_{{\bm{r}}}G_{r},\quad G_{r}=\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}\nu_{\bm{s}}\left[g_{2\bm{s}}-\left(\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}g^{*}\right)_{2\bm{s}}\right]=\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}\nu_{\bm{s}}\left[g_{2\bm{s}}-\left(\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}g^{*}\right)_{2\bm{s}}\right].

Consequently, we have

\dot{\beta}_{{\bm{m}}}=-\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}\mathcal{L}\cdot\partialderivative{g_{\mm}}{\beta_{\mm}}=-\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}G_{r},

and thus

\dot{g}_{{\bm{m}}}=\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\dot{\beta}_{{\bm{m}}}=-\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}G_{r},\qquad\dot{h}_{r}=-\mu_{2r}G_{r},

where we recall that
\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}=\mu_{\absolutevalue{\mm}}=\mu_{2r}
.
Let us further compute
G_{r}
.
Proposition
E.3
gives that

\left(\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}g^{*}\right)_{2\bm{s}}=\sigma^{2\bm{s}}g^{*}_{2\bm{s}}=\sigma^{2\bm{s}}\nu_{\bm{s}}h_{r}^{*}.

so

\displaystyle G_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}\nu_{\bm{s}}\left[\nu_{\bm{s}}h_{r}-\sigma^{2\bm{s}}\nu_{\bm{s}}h_{r}^{*}\right]=\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}\nu_{\bm{s}}^{2}h_{r}-\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}\nu_{\bm{s}}^{2}\sigma^{2\bm{s}}h_{r}^{*}

\displaystyle=h_{r}-\sum_{\bm{s}:\absolutevalue{\bm{s}}=r}\nu_{\bm{s}}^{2}\sigma^{2\bm{s}}h_{r}^{*}

Let us introduce

\phi_{r}\coloneqq\sum_{{\bm{r}}:\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}

(121)

Consequently, we finally get

\dot{h}_{r}=\mu_{2r}\left[\phi_{r}h_{r}^{*}-h_{r}\right].

The dynamics of
W
.

Recall that we have
\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\norm{g}_{\gamma_{p}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\norm{g^*}_{\gamma_{p^{*}}}^{2}-\left\langle{P_{W}g,f^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}
.
Therefore, using Lemma
E.4
, we have

\displaystyle\dot{W}=-\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\left\langle{P_{W}g,f^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}={\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}\int(\nabla f^{*})(x)(P_{W}\nabla g)^{\top}\differential\gamma_{d}(x)

\displaystyle={\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}\int W_{*}(P_{W_{*}}(\nabla g^{*}))(P_{W}\nabla g)^{\top}\differential\gamma_{d}(x)

\displaystyle={\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}W_{*}B^{\top},

where

B=\int P_{W_{*}}(P_{W}\nabla g)(P_{W_{*}}(\nabla g^{*}))^{\top}\differential\gamma_{d}(x).

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\dot{\varPsi}

\displaystyle=\dot{W}^{{\top}}W_{*}=({\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}W_{*}B^{\top})^{{\top}}W_{*}=\left[W_{*}B^{\top}-W\Sym(W^{\top}W_{*}B^{\top})\right]^{{\top}}W_{*}

\displaystyle=B-\Sym(\varPsi B^{\top})\varPsi.

The dynamics of
\Sigma

.
To compute the dynamics of
\Sigma
, we use (
105
) to get

\displaystyle\dot{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\Diag(U^{\top}\dot{\varPsi}V)=\Diag(U^{\top}(B-\Sym(\varPsi B^{\top})\varPsi)V)=\Diag(U^{\top}BV)-\Diag(U^{\top}\Sym(\varPsi B^{\top})\varPsi V)

\displaystyle=\Diag(U^{\top}BV)-\frac{1}{2}\Diag(U^{\top}U\Sigma V^{\top}B^{\top}U\Sigma V^{\top}V)-\frac{1}{2}\Diag(U^{\top}B\varPsi^{\top}\varPsi V)

\displaystyle=\Diag(U^{\top}BV)-\frac{1}{2}\Diag(\Sigma V^{{\top}}B^{{\top}}U\Sigma)-\frac{1}{2}\Diag(U^{\top}BV\Sigma^{2}).

Now, let us define
\tilde{B}=U^{\top}BV\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\dot{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\Diag(\tilde{B})-\frac{1}{2}\Diag(\Sigma\tilde{B}^{{\top}}\Sigma)-\frac{1}{2}\Diag(\tilde{B}\Sigma^{2})

\displaystyle=\Diag(\tilde{B})-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma\Diag(\tilde{B}^{{\top}})\Sigma-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma^{2}\Diag(\tilde{B})

\displaystyle=\Diag(\tilde{B})-\Sigma^{2}\Diag(\tilde{B})

\displaystyle=(I_{p}-\Sigma^{2})\Diag(\tilde{B}).

It remains to compute
\Diag(\tilde{B})
.
Using the SVD and Proposition
E.2
, we can write
B
as

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\left\langle{P_{W}\nabla g,P_{W_{*}}\nabla g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\left\langle{\nabla g,P_{W}^{\top}P_{W_{*}}(\nabla g^{*})^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}=\left\langle{\nabla g,\mathcal{A}_{\varPsi}(\nabla g^{*})^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\nabla g,\mathcal{A}_{U}\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}_{V^{\top}}(\nabla g^{*})^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\mathcal{A}_{U^{\top}}\nabla g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}_{V^{\top}}(\nabla g^{*})^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}.

Hence, we get

\displaystyle\tilde{B}

\displaystyle=U^{\top}BV=U^{\top}\left\langle{\mathcal{A}_{U^{\top}}\nabla g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}_{V^{\top}}(\nabla g^{*})^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}V=\left\langle{U^{\top}\mathcal{A}_{U^{\top}}\nabla g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\mathcal{A}_{V^{\top}}(V^{\top}\nabla g^{*})^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\left\langle{\nabla(\mathcal{A}_{U^{\top}}g),\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\nabla(\mathcal{A}_{V^{\top}}g^{*})^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\left\langle{\nabla(P_{U^{\top}}g),\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}(\nabla(P_{V^{\top}}g^{*}))^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\left\langle{\nabla g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}(\nabla g^{*})^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}},

where we use (
104
) in Lemma
E.5
to get (a),
Proposition
E.2
to get (b),
and the rotation invariance of
g
and
g^{*}
to get (c).
Finally, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{B}_{ii}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\nabla_{x_{i}}g,\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\nabla_{x_{i}}g^{*}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}=\left\langle{\sum\sqrt{m_{i}+1}g_{{\bm{m}}+e_{i}}H_{{\bm{m}}},\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}\sum\sqrt{n_{i}+1}g^{*}_{{\bm{n}}+e_{i}}H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum\sqrt{(n_{i}+1)(m_{i}+1)}g_{{\bm{m}}+e_{i}}g^{*}_{{\bm{n}}+e_{i}}\left\langle{H_{{\bm{m}}},\mathcal{A}_{\Sigma}H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum\sqrt{(n_{i}+1)(m_{i}+1)}g_{{\bm{m}}+e_{i}}g^{*}_{{\bm{n}}+e_{i}}\left\langle{H_{{\bm{m}}},\sigma^{{\bm{n}}}H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{m}}}(m_{i}+1)\sigma^{{\bm{m}}}g_{{\bm{m}}+e_{i}}g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}+e_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{m}}}m_{i}\sigma^{{\bm{m}}-e_{i}}g_{{\bm{m}}}g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}2r_{i}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}-e_{i}}\nu_{\bm{r}}^{2},

and thus

\dot{\sigma}_{i}=(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}2r_{i}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}-e_{i}}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2},

and

\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}=2\sigma_{i}\dot{\sigma}_{i}=4(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}.

The dynamics of
\phi_{r}
and
\omega

For
\phi_{r}
, we have

\displaystyle\derivative{t}\phi_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\derivative{t}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sigma^{2({\bm{r}}-e_{i})}2r_{i}\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}

For
\omega
, let us recall that

\omega\coloneqq-\frac{1}{K}\log(\sum_{i=1}^p e^{-K \sigma_i^2}).

Using Proposition
F.7
, we get

\displaystyle\dot{\omega}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\partialderivative{\omega}{\sigma_i^2}\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\frac{e^{-K\sigma_{j}}}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\cdot(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}

Summary

Collecting the results, we have the following proposition.

Proposition E.15
.

Consider the population dynamics (
119
).
Then, we have the following dynamics:

\displaystyle\dot{h}_{r}

\displaystyle=\mu_{2r}\left(\phi_{r}h_{r}^{*}-h_{r}\right)

(122)

\displaystyle\dot{W}

\displaystyle={\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}W_{*}B^{\top},\quad B=\left\langle{P_{W}\nabla g,(P_{W_{*}}\nabla g^{*})^{\top}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}

\displaystyle\dot{\varPsi}

\displaystyle=B-\Sym(\varPsi B^{\top})\varPsi

\displaystyle\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=4(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\dot{\phi}_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sigma^{2({\bm{r}}-e_{i})}2r_{i}\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2},\quad\dot{\phi}_{1}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\dot{\omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}}\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}

E.3.2
Analysis of the Dynamics

Proposition E.16
.

Consider the population dynamics (
119
)
Suppose
\rho(0)\neq 0
.
Then, for all
r\geq 0,t\geq 0
, we have

h_{r}^{*}h_{r}(t)\geq 0,\qquad\dot{\phi}_{r}(t)\geq 0,\quad\dot{\omega}\geq 0.

Moreover, we have the bound

\displaystyle\dot{\omega}

\displaystyle\geq C(1-\phi_{1})\sum_{r\geq 1}r\omega^{r}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}

(123)

\displaystyle\dot{\phi}_{1}

\displaystyle\geq C(1-\phi_{1})\sum_{r\geq 1}r\omega^{r}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}

\displaystyle\dot{h}_{r}

\displaystyle\geq\mu_{2r}\left(\omega^{r}h_{r}^{*}-h_{r}\right),\quad\text{(assuming $h_{r}^{*}>0$)}

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
h_{r}^{*}
is positive.
To prove the first statement, we observe that if
\phi_{r}(t)\geq 0
, then
h_{r}
will be non-negative.
Then, the dynamics of
\phi_{r}
shows that
\dot{\phi}_{r}\geq 0
,
which in turn guarantees that
h_{r}
is non-negative.
A rigorous proof can be made by a standard contradiction argument in the ODE theory.

For the second statement, first we have

\displaystyle\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=4(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}\geq 4(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\omega^{\absolutevalue{\rr}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}4(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\omega^{r}\frac{r}{p}=\frac{4}{p}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}r\omega^{r}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})A,\quad A=\frac{4}{p}\sum_{r\geq 1}r\omega^{r}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}

where we apply (
114
) in (a).
Plugging this into the dynamics of
\phi_{r}
, we have

\displaystyle\dot{\phi}_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sigma^{2({\bm{r}}-e_{i})}2r_{i}\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}\geq\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\omega^{\absolutevalue{\rr}-1}2r_{i}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})A

\displaystyle=A\omega^{r-1}\sum_{i=1}^{p}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}2r_{i}=A\omega^{r-1}\sum_{i=1}^{p}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\frac{2r}{p}

\displaystyle=2rA(1-\phi_{1})\omega^{r-1}.

Particularly,

\dot{\phi}_{1}\geq C(1-\phi_{1})\sum_{r\geq 1}r\omega^{r}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}.

For
\dot{\omega}
, we have

\displaystyle\dot{\omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}\derivative{t}\sigma_{i}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})A

\displaystyle\geq(1-\phi_{1})A

where we apply Proposition
F.8
in the last inequality.

∎

Proposition E.17
.

Consider the population dynamics (
119
).
Let Assumption
5
hold and
{r_{0}}=m_{0}/2
.
Suppose
\rho_{0}=\min_{1\leq j\leq p}\sigma_{j}(0)\neq 0
.
Then, by taking
K\geq 2\rho_{0}^{-1}\log p
, we have

\forall t\geq T^{\mathrm{app}}
,

\min_{1\leq j\leq p}\absolutevalue{\sigma_j(t)}\geq\frac{1}{2},\quad\absolutevalue{h_{\rz}(t)}\geq 2^{-(m_{0}+1)}\absolutevalue{h^*_{\rz}},

(124)

where

T^{\mathrm{app}}\lesssim\log\rho_{0}^{-1}+\rho_{0}^{-2(m_{0}-1)}.

(125)

Proof.

We can focus on the
t
such that
\phi_{1}(t)\leq 1-\frac{1}{2p}
:
If
\phi_{1}(t)\geq 1-\frac{1}{2p}
, then since
p\phi_{1}(t)\leq p-1+\sigma_{j}^{2}
, we already have
\sigma_{j}^{2}\geq 1/2.

Using the property in Proposition
F.7
of
\omega
, as long as we take
K\geq 2\rho_{0}^{-1}\log p
, we have

\omega(0)\geq\rho_{0}-\frac{1}{K}\log p\geq\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0}.

Now, we can use the dynamics of
\dot{\omega}
to get

\dot{\omega}\geq cp^{-2}\sum_{r\geq 1}r\omega^{r}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\geq c\omega^{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}h^{*}_{{r_{0}}},

while

\dot{h}_{{r_{0}}}\geq c\left(\omega^{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}-h_{{r_{0}}}\right).

Therefore, we can follow the same idea of analysis as in Proposition
D.4
.
Let us define

T_{0}^{\rho}=0,\quad T_{k}^{g}=\inf{\left\{t\geq T_{k}^{\rho}:h_{{r_{0}}}(t)\geq\frac{1}{2}\rho_{k}^{2{r_{0}}}h^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\right\}},\quad T_{k}^{\rho}=\inf{\left\{t\geq T_{k-1}^{g}:\omega(t)\geq\rho_{k}^{2}\right\}},

(126)

where
\rho_{k}=2^{k}\rho_{0}
and
k\leq L
,
L\coloneqq 1+\lceil\log_{2}\rho_{0}^{-1}\rceil
.
When
t\in[T_{k}^{\rho},T_{k}^{g}]
,
we have

\dot{h}_{{r_{0}}}\geq\frac{1}{2}c\rho_{k}^{2{r_{0}}}h^{*}_{{r_{0}}}=c\rho_{k}^{2{r_{0}}}h^{*}_{{r_{0}}}

so

T_{k}^{g}-T_{k}^{\rho}\leq C.

On the other hand, when
t\in[T_{k-1}^{g},T_{k}^{g}]
, we have

\dot{\omega}\geq c\rho_{k}^{2{r_{0}}}\cdot\rho_{k}^{2{r_{0}}}h^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\cdot h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}=c\rho_{k}^{4{r_{0}}}(h^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{2}.

Hence,

T_{k}^{\rho}-T_{k-1}^{g}\leq\frac{\rho_{k}^{2}-\rho_{k-1}^{2}}{\rho_{k}^{4{r_{0}}}(h^{*}_{{r_{0}}})^{2}}=C\rho_{0}^{-2(2{r_{0}}-1)}2^{-2(2{r_{0}}-1)k}.

Consequently,

T_{L}^{\rho}\leq\sum_{k\leq L}\left[(T_{k}^{\rho}-T_{k-1}^{g})+(T_{k-1}^{g}-T_{k-1}^{\rho})\right]\lesssim\log\rho_{0}^{-1}+\rho_{0}^{-2(2{r_{0}}-1)}.

Recalling that
m_{0}=2{r_{0}}
, we obtain the desired bound.
∎

Proposition E.18
.

Consider the population dynamics (
119
).
Let Assumption
5
hold and
{r_{0}}=m_{0}/2
.
Suppose that (
124
) holds for some
t_{0}
.
Then,

1-\phi_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp(-c t),\quad\forall j=1,\dots,p.

(127)

Proof.

When the condition (
124
) holds, we have

\dot{\phi}_{1}\geq C(1-\phi_{1})\sum_{r\geq 1}r\omega^{r}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\geq C(1-\phi_{1}){r_{0}}\omega^{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}h^{*}_{{r_{0}}}\geq c(1-\phi_{1}).

∎

Proof of Theorem
2.7
.

The monotonicity of the feature error measure follows from (
116
) and the monotonicity of
\sigma_{j}
in Proposition
E.16
.
For the initialization, from Proposition
E.6
we have

\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{d}}\leq\sigma_{j}(0)\leq\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{d}},\quad\forall j=1,\dots,p.

with high probability.
Therefore, we have

\mathcal{E}_{\text{Proj}}(\Phi_{W(0)},f^{*})=\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}(1-\sigma^{2{\bm{m}}})(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2},\gtrsim\sum_{{\bm{m}}\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}(g^{*}_{{\bm{m}}})^{2}\gtrsim 1.

On the other hand, since
\rho_{0}=\min_{j}\sigma_{j}(0)\geq c_{1}/\sqrt{d}
, Proposition
E.17
and Proposition
E.18
shows that for some
t_{0}\asymp\log d+d^{{r_{0}}-1}
, we have

1-\absolutevalue{\sigma_j(t_0 + t)}\leq\frac{1}{2}\exp(-C t),\quad\forall j=1,\dots,p.

Hence, the result follow from applying Proposition
E.14
and adjusting the constants.
∎

E.4
Sequence Model

E.4.1
Computing the Dynamics

Let us first compute the dynamics of the adaptive kernel model.
We modify the computations in Subsection
E.3.1
with (
142
) for the computation.

Dynamics of
h_{r}
.

First, using (
142
) and Corollary
E.9
, we have

-\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=-\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}\mathcal{L}+\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}f_{{\bm{n}}}=-\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}\mathcal{L}+\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}\nabla_{h_{r}}f_{{\bm{n}}},

where
{\bm{m}}=2{\bm{r}}
,
r=\absolutevalue{\rr}
, and

\nabla_{h_{r}}f_{{\bm{n}}}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}\nabla_{g_{2{\bm{r}}}}f_{{\bm{n}}},\quad f_{{\bm{n}}}\coloneqq\left\langle{f,H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\left\langle{P_{W}g,H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}.

Therefore, we have

\dot{\beta}_{{\bm{m}}}=-\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\cdot\partialderivative{g_{\mm}}{\beta_{\mm}}=\lambda_{{\bm{m}}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left[-\nabla_{g_{{\bm{m}}}}^{\mathcal{G}(p)}\mathcal{L}+\nu_{{\bm{r}}}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\nabla_{h_{r}}f_{{\bm{n}}}\right],

and thus

\dot{h}_{r}=\mu_{2r}\left(-G_{r}+e_{r}\right),\quad e_{r}=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\nabla_{h_{r}}f_{{\bm{n}}}.

Let us further compute
e_{r}
.
First, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{h_{r}}f_{{\bm{n}}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{h_{r}}\left\langle{P_{W}g,H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\nabla_{h_{r}}\left\langle{g,P_{W}^{\top}H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{h_{r}}\left\langle{\sum_{s\geq 0}h_{s}\sum_{\bm{s}=s}\nu_{\bm{s}}H_{2\bm{s}},P_{W}^{\top}H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{h_{r}}\sum_{s\geq 0}h_{s}\sum_{\bm{s}=s}\nu_{\bm{s}}\left\langle{H_{2\bm{s}},P_{W}^{\top}H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}\left\langle{H_{2{\bm{r}}},P_{W}^{\top}H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}.

Plugging this into
e_{r}
, we get

\displaystyle e_{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}\left\langle{H_{2{\bm{r}}},P_{W}^{\top}H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\left\langle{P_{W}\left(\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}H_{2{\bm{r}}}\right),H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\left\langle{P_{W}\bar{H}_{r},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}},

where
\bar{H}_{r}=\sum_{{\bm{r}}:\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}H_{2{\bm{r}}}
.

Dynamics of
\Sigma
.

Using (
142
), we have

\dot{W}=-\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=-\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\mathcal{L}+\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}f_{{\bm{n}}},

Consequently, following the computation in the population case, we have
\dot{\varPsi}=\dot{W}^{{\top}}W_{*}

and

\dot{\Sigma}=\Diag(U^{\top}\dot{\varPsi}V)=(I_{p}-\Sigma^{2})\Diag(\tilde{B})+\Diag(U^{\top}(\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}f_{{\bm{n}}})^{\top}W_{*}V).

The last term is the error term that we need to analyze.
Let us denote

\Delta\coloneqq\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}(\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}f_{{\bm{n}}})^{\top}W_{*}V.

First, recalling the definition of
f_{{\bm{n}}}
and (
96
), we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}f_{{\bm{n}}}=\nabla_{W}^{{\mathcal{S}}}\left\langle{P_{W}g,H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}={\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}\nabla_{W}\left\langle{P_{W}g,H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}={\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}Z_{{\bm{n}}},

where we denote
Z_{{\bm{n}}}=\nabla_{W}\left\langle{P_{W}g,H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}
.
Expanding
{\Pi}_{T_{W}{\mathcal{S}}}Z_{{\bm{n}}}
with (
96
), we have

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}\left[Z_{{\bm{n}}}-W\Sym(W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}})\right]^{\top}W_{*}V

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}\left[Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}-\frac{1}{2}(W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}+Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W)W^{\top}\right]W_{*}V.

Now, let us write

\displaystyle W_{*}=WQ+E,\quad Q\coloneqq UV^{{\top}},\quad E\coloneqq W_{*}-WQ

we have

\displaystyle W^{{\top}}E=W^{{\top}}W_{*}-Q=U\Sigma V^{{\top}}-UV^{{\top}}=U(\Sigma-I_{p})V^{{\top}}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}\left[Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}-\frac{1}{2}(W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}+Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W)W^{\top}\right](WU+EV)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}\left[Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W-\frac{1}{2}(W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}+Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W)W^{\top}W\right]U+\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}\left[Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}EV-\frac{1}{2}(W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}+Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W)W^{\top}EV\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}\left[\frac{1}{2}Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W-\frac{1}{2}W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}\right]U+\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}\left[Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}EV-\frac{1}{2}(W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}+Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W)U(\Sigma-I_{p})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}\left[\frac{1}{2}Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W-\frac{1}{2}W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}\right]U+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}\left[W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}+Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W\right]U(I-\Sigma)+\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}U^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}EV

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{n}}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\left(\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(0)}+\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(1)}+\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(2)}\right),

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}U^{\top}\left[\frac{1}{2}Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W-\frac{1}{2}W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}\right]U,

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}U^{\top}\left[W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}+Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W\right]U(I-\Sigma),

\displaystyle\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=U^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}EV.

Now, let us further introduce

\tilde{W}=WU,\quad\tilde{W}_{*}=W_{*}V=WU+EV,\quad\tilde{E}=\tilde{W}_{*}-\tilde{W}=W_{*}V-WU,\quad\tilde{Z}_{{\bm{n}}}\coloneqq Z_{{\bm{n}}}U.

(128)

For
\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(0)}
, it is easy to find that

\displaystyle\left(\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(0)}\right)^{{\top}}=-\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(0)},

so the diagonal of
\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(0)}
is zero:

\Diag(\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(0)})=0.

For
\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(1)}
, since
\frac{1}{2}U^{\top}\left[W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}+Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W\right]U=\Sym(U^{\top}W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)
, we have

\displaystyle\Diag(\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Diag(U^{\top}\left(W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}+Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}W\right)U)(I-\Sigma)

\displaystyle=\Diag(U^{\top}W^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)(I-\Sigma)

\displaystyle=\Diag((I-\Sigma)\tilde{W}^{\top}\tilde{Z}_{{\bm{n}}})

\displaystyle=\Diag((\tilde{W}(I-\Sigma))^{\top}\tilde{Z}_{{\bm{n}}}).

The last term
\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(2)}
can be written as

\displaystyle\Diag(\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(2)})

\displaystyle=\Diag(U^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}^{\top}EV)=\Diag((Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)^{\top}(W_{*}-WQ)V)=\Diag((Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)^{\top}(W_{*}V-WU))

\displaystyle=\Diag((\tilde{W}_{*}-\tilde{W})^{\top}\tilde{Z}_{{\bm{n}}}).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\Diag(\Delta_{{\bm{n}}})

\displaystyle=\Diag(\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(0)}+\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(1)}+\Delta_{{\bm{n}}}^{(2)})

\displaystyle=\Diag((\tilde{W}(I-\Sigma))^{\top}\tilde{Z}_{{\bm{n}}})+\Diag((\tilde{W}_{*}-\tilde{W})^{\top}\tilde{Z}_{{\bm{n}}})

\displaystyle=\Diag\left[(\tilde{W}(I-\Sigma)+\tilde{W}_{*}-\tilde{W})^{\top}\tilde{Z}_{{\bm{n}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Diag\left((\tilde{W}_{*}-\tilde{W}\Sigma)^{\top}\tilde{Z}_{{\bm{n}}}\right).

Substituting back to
W,W_{*}
, we find that

\displaystyle\tilde{W}_{*}-\tilde{W}\Sigma

\displaystyle=W_{*}V-WU\Sigma=(W_{*}-WU\Sigma V^{{\top}})V=(W_{*}-W\varPsi)V=(W_{*}-WW^{{\top}}W_{*})V

\displaystyle=P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*}V,

where
P_{W}^{\perp}=I-WW^{{\top}}
is the projection orthogonal to
W
.
Hence,

\Diag(\Delta_{\bm{n}})=\Diag\left((\tilde{W}_{*}-\tilde{W}\Sigma)^{\top}\tilde{Z}_{{\bm{n}}}\right)=\Diag(V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U).

In summary, we obtain that

\dot{\sigma}_{i}=(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}2r_{i}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}-e_{i}}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}+\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\Diag(V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)_{ii},

and also

\displaystyle\derivative{t}\dot{\sigma}_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=4(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}+\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\Diag(\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)_{ii}

\displaystyle=(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})A_{i}+\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\Diag(\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)_{ii},

where
A_{i}=4\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}
.

Dynamics of
\phi_{1}
and
\omega

Using the chain rule, we can compute the dynamics of
\phi_{1}
and
\omega
.
It suffices to focus on the noise term.
Let us introduce
M=\varPsi^{{\top}}\varPsi
.
For
\phi_{1}
, the noise term writes

\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\Diag(\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)_{ii}=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\Tr\left[\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U\right].

While for
\omega
, using the chain rule with Proposition
F.7
, we have

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{i}^{2}}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\Diag(\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)_{ii}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\Diag(e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U)_{ii}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\Tr\left[e^{-K\Sigma^{2}}\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U\right],

where
e^{-K\Sigma^{2}}
represents a matrix function.
Moreover, the denominator can be written as

\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}=\Tr e^{-K\Sigma^{2}}=\Tr\exp(-K M)

is independent of the SVD decomposition of
\varPsi
.

Therefore, let us consider in general a function
\varphi
and the noise term in the form of

\chi=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\Tr\left[\varphi(\Sigma^{2})\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U\right]=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}T_{{\bm{n}}}.

Then, we can express
T_{{\bm{n}}}
as

\displaystyle T_{{\bm{n}}}

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\varphi(\Sigma^{2})\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}Z_{{\bm{n}}}U\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\Tr\left[U\varphi(\Sigma^{2})\Sigma V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}\left\langle{\nabla_{W}(P_{W}g),H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\Tr\left[U\Sigma\varphi(\Sigma^{2})V^{\top}(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}\left\langle{x(P_{W}\nabla g)^{\top},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\left\langle{\Tr\left[\varPsi\varphi(M)(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}x(P_{W}\nabla g)^{\top}\right],H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\left\langle{{(P_{W}\nabla g)^{\top}W^{T}W_{*}\varphi(M)(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}x},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{=}}\left\langle{{(\nabla_{x}(P_{W}g))^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M)(P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*})^{\top}x},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle{{(\nabla_{x}(P_{W}g))^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}x},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle,

where we use the commutative property of the trace in
(a,d)
,
gradient formula Lemma
E.4
in
(b,e)
,
and matrix calculus (
107
) in
(c)
.
We note here that the final result is independent of the singular value decomposition.

Summary.

We summarize the results in the following proposition.

Proposition E.19
.

Consider the dynamics (
28
) under Assumption
4
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\dot{h}_{r}

\displaystyle=\mu_{2r}\left[\phi_{r}h_{r}^{*}-h_{r}+e_{r}\right],

(129)

\displaystyle\dot{\omega}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{i}}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})A_{i}+\zeta

\displaystyle\dot{\phi}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})A_{i}+\xi

where
A_{i}=4\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}},

and the noise terms are given by

\displaystyle e_{r}

\displaystyle=e_{r}(W)=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\left\langle{P_{W}\bar{H}_{r},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}},

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=\zeta(W,g)=\frac{1}{\Tr\exp(-K M)}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\left\langle{{(\nabla_{x}(P_{W}g))^{\top}W_{*}e^{-KM}W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}x},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=\xi(W,g)=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\left\langle{{(\nabla_{x}(P_{W}g))^{\top}W_{*}W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}x},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle,

where
M=\varPsi^{\top}\varPsi
.

E.4.2
Bounding the perturbation terms

Let us now apply Lemma
F.3
to bound the perturbation terms in Proposition
E.19
.

Proposition E.20
.

Let
e_{r}
be defined in Proposition
E.19
.
Then,

\kappa(W,W^{\prime})\coloneqq\mathrm{Cov}(e_{r}(W),e_{r}(W^{\prime}))=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{r}^{2}\lambda^{2{\bm{r}}},

where
\lambda
is the singular values of
W^{\top}W^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
\kappa(W,W^{\prime})
is Lipschitz with respect to the Euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}
with Lipschitz constant
2r
.
Hence, with probability at least
1-4\exp(-d)
, we have

\sup_{w\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\absolutevalue{e_r(w)}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{dp\log r}{n}},\quad\forall r\geq 0.

(130)

Proof.

Recall that
\bar{H}_{r}
is a rotation invariant polynomial of degree
2r
from Lemma
E.7
.
We can compute the covariance function of
e_{r}(W)
:

\displaystyle\kappa(W,W^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cov}(e_{r}(W),e_{r}(W^{\prime}))=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\left\langle{P_{W}\bar{H}_{r},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}\left\langle{P_{W^{\prime}}\bar{H}_{r},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left\langle{P_{W}\bar{H}_{r},P_{W^{\prime}}\bar{H}_{r}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\left\langle{\bar{H}_{r},P_{W}^{\top}P_{W^{\prime}}\bar{H}_{r}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left\langle{\bar{H}_{r},\mathcal{A}_{W^{\top}W^{\prime}}\bar{H}_{r}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}.

Let us consider the SVD
W^{\top}W^{\prime}=Q_{1}\Lambda Q_{2}^{\top}
and let
\lambda
be the diagonal entries of
\Lambda
.
Then,

\displaystyle\kappa(W,W^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left\langle{\bar{H}_{r},\mathcal{A}_{Q_{1}\Lambda Q_{2}^{\top}}\bar{H}_{r}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}=\frac{1}{n}\left\langle{\bar{H}_{r},\mathcal{A}_{Q_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}\mathcal{A}_{Q_{2}^{\top}}\bar{H}_{r}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left\langle{\bar{H}_{r},\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda}\bar{H}_{r}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{r}^{2}\lambda^{2{\bm{r}}}.

Let us now view
\kappa
as a binary function on
{\left\{W\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}:\norm{W}\leq 1\right\}}
with the last expression.
We can compute the derivative of
k
with respect to
W
.
Take a tangent direction
H\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}
, we use (
106
) to obtain

D_{H}\Lambda=\Diag(U^{{\top}}D_{H}(W^{\top}W^{\prime})V)=\Diag(U^{{\top}}H^{\top}W^{\prime}V),\qquad D_{H}\lambda_{j}=u_{j}^{{\top}}H^{\top}W^{\prime}v_{j},

so

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{D_H \lambda_j}\leq\norm{H}\norm{W'}\leq\norm{H}.

Then, using

D_{H}\lambda^{2{\bm{r}}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}2r_{j}\lambda^{2{\bm{r}}-e_{j}}D_{H}\lambda_{j},

we have

\absolutevalue{D_H \lambda^{2\rr}}\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{p}r_{j}\lambda^{2{\bm{r}}-e_{j}}\absolutevalue{D_H \lambda_j}\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{p}r_{j}\norm{H}=2\absolutevalue{\rr}\norm{H}.

Finally,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{D_{H} k(W,W')}

\displaystyle=\absolutevalue{\sum_{\abs{\rr} = r} \nu_{\rr}^2 D_{H} \lambda^{2\rr}}\leq\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\absolutevalue{D_{H} \lambda^{2\rr}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\absolutevalue{\rr}\norm{H}\leq 2r\norm{H}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2r\norm{H}\leq 2r\norm{H}_{2}.

Therefore, we find that

\norm{D k(W,W')}_{2}\leq 2r,

which implies that
k(W,W^{\prime})
is Lipchitz with respect to the Euclidean norm.
Applying Lemma
F.3
, we obtained the desired bound.
∎

Before bounding
\xi
and
\zeta
, let us first make some preliminary computation.
Let us consider a noise term in the form of

\chi=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\left\langle{{(\nabla_{x}(P_{W}\bar{H}_{r}))^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}x},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle.

We can decompose
g
as

g=\sum_{r\geq 0}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\mm}=2\bm{r},\absolutevalue{r}=r}g_{{\bm{m}}}H_{{\bm{m}}}=\sum_{r\geq 0}h_{r}\bar{H}_{r},\quad\bar{H}_{r}=\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}H_{2{\bm{r}}}.

Then, we have

\chi=\sum_{r\geq 0}h_{r}\chi_{r},\quad\chi_{r}=\sum_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\varepsilon_{{\bm{n}}}\left\langle{{(\nabla_{x}(P_{W}\bar{H}_{r}))^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}x},H_{{\bm{n}}}}\right\rangle,

where
\chi_{r}
is independent of
g
.

Let us take
W^{\prime}
and denote the corresponding quantities with a prime (such as
\varPsi^{\prime},M^{\prime}
).
We compute the covariance of
\chi_{r}(W)
and
\chi_{r}(W^{\prime})
.

\displaystyle k_{\chi,r}(W,W^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cov}(\chi_{r}(W),\chi_{r}(W^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\left\langle{{(\nabla_{x}(P_{W}\bar{H}_{r}))^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}x},{(\nabla_{x}(P_{W^{\prime}}\bar{H}_{r}))^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M^{\prime})W_{*}^{\top}P_{W^{\prime}}^{\perp}x}}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{d}}

where we use the fact that
(H_{{\bm{n}}})_{{\bm{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is an orthogonal basis.
Noticing that

P_{W}^{\perp}\nabla_{x}(P_{W}\bar{H}_{r})=P_{W}^{\perp}WP_{W}(\nabla\bar{H}_{r})=0,

we can use Lemma
C.7
with
f=P_{W}\bar{H}_{r}
,
A=W_{*}\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}

to get

k_{\chi,r}(W,W^{\prime})=k_{\chi,r}^{(1)}(W,W^{\prime})+k_{\chi,r}^{(2)}(W,W^{\prime}),

where

\displaystyle k_{\chi,r}^{(1)}(W,W^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\nabla_{x}(P_{W}\bar{H}_{r}))^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}(W_{*}\varphi(M^{\prime})W_{*}^{\top}P_{W^{\prime}}^{\perp})^{{\top}}\nabla_{x}(P_{W^{\prime}}\bar{H}_{r})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(P_{W}\nabla_{x}\bar{H}_{r})^{\top}W^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}P_{W^{\prime}}^{\perp}W_{*}\varphi(M^{\prime})W_{*}^{\top}W^{\prime}(P_{W^{\prime}}\nabla_{x}\bar{H}_{r})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(P_{W}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{\top}\varPsi\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}P_{W^{\prime}}^{\perp}W_{*}\varphi(M^{\prime})(\varPsi^{\prime})^{\top}(P_{W^{\prime}}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})

and

\displaystyle k_{\chi,r}^{(2)}(W,W^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Tr\nabla_{x}^{2}(P_{W}\bar{H}_{r})\left(W_{*}\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}\right)\nabla_{x}^{2}(P_{W^{\prime}}\bar{H}_{r})(W_{*}\varphi(M^{\prime})W_{*}^{\top}P_{W^{\prime}}^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Tr W(P_{W}\nabla^{2}\bar{H}_{r}){W^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}}W^{\prime}(P_{W^{\prime}}\nabla^{2}\bar{H}_{r})(W^{\prime})^{\top}W_{*}\varphi(M^{\prime})W_{*}^{\top}P_{W^{\prime}}^{\perp}.

We can further compute the variance.
We find that
P_{W}^{\perp}W^{\prime}=0
when
W=W^{\prime}
, so

\displaystyle k_{\chi,r}(W,W)

\displaystyle=k_{\chi,r}^{(1)}(W,W)=\mathbb{E}(P_{W}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{\top}\varPsi\varphi(M)W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}W_{*}\varphi(M)\varPsi^{\top}(P_{W}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(P_{W}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{\top}\varPsi\varphi(M)(I-M)\varphi(M)\varPsi^{\top}(P_{W}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\Tr\varPsi\varphi(M)(I-M)\varphi(M)\varPsi\left[\mathbb{E}(\nabla\bar{H}_{r})(\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{{\top}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2r}{p}\Tr\varPsi\varphi(M)(I-M)\varphi(M)\varPsi^{\top}

\displaystyle=\frac{2r}{p}\Tr\varphi(M)^{2}M(I-M).

Proposition E.21
.

With probability at least
1-4\exp(-d)
, it holds that for any
W\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
satisfying

\phi_{1}(W)\leq 1-\nu
and any
g
,

\absolutevalue{\xi(W,g)}\lesssim(1-\phi_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{pd}{n}}\sqrt{\log(p\nu^{-1})}\sum_{r\geq 1}(r\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{h_r}.

(131)

Proof.

Taking
\varphi\equiv 1
in the previous computation, we find that

k_{\xi,r}(W,W)=\frac{2r}{p}\Tr(I- \Sigma^2)\Sigma^{2}\leq\frac{2r}{p}\Tr(I- \Sigma^2)=2r(1-\phi_{1}).

To bound the derivative of
k_{\xi,r}(W,W^{\prime})
, we can use
\Tr A\leq p\norm{A}
and the chain rule of derivative iteratively.
The quantities
\varPsi=W^{\top}W_{*}
and
P_{W}^{\top}=I-WW^{\top}
are all polynomial in
W
.
Also, we recall that
\nabla_{W}(P_{W}h)=xP_{W}(\nabla g)^{{\top}}
.
Combining this with the derivative formula of Hermite polynomials and that
\norm{W}\leq 1
,
the derivative of each entry in
P_{W}(\nabla^{2}\bar{H}_{r})
is bounded by a polynomial of degree at most
2r+1
with coefficients being a polynomial of
r
,
so its
L^{2}(\gamma_{d})
norm is also bounded by a polynomial of
r
.
Consequently, we can deduce that
\norm{\nabla_W k_{\xi,r}(W,W')}_{2}
is bounded by some polynomial in
p,r
.
Moreover, since

\phi_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sigma_{i}^{2}=\Tr\varPsi^{{\top}}\varPsi,

the derivative of
\phi_{1}
is also bounded by a polynomial in
p,r
.
Let us take
\bar{\xi}=\frac{1}{2r}(1-\phi_{1})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\xi
and
the scaled covariance

\bar{k}_{\xi,r}(W,W^{\prime})\coloneqq\frac{1}{2r}\left[(1-\phi_{1})(1-\phi_{1}^{\prime})\right]^{-\frac{1}{2}}k_{\xi,r}(W,W^{\prime}).

Then, as long as
1-\phi_{1}\geq\nu
,
the derivative of
\bar{k}_{\xi,r}(W,W^{\prime})
is bounded by some polynomial of
p,r,\nu^{-1}
.
Finally, we can apply Lemma
F.3
together with a union bound to obtain that

\absolutevalue{\bar{\xi}_r}\lesssim\sqrt{\log(r p \nu^{-1})+C}\sqrt{pd},\quad\forall r\geq 1

with probability at least
1-4\exp(-d)
.
Returning to
\xi_{r}
and taking a summation over
r
yields the desired bound.
∎

Proposition E.22
.

With probability at least
1-4\exp(-d)
, it holds that for any
W\in{\mathcal{S}}(d,p)
and any
g
,

\absolutevalue{\zeta(W,g)}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{pd}{n}}\sqrt{\log(pK)}\sum_{r\geq 1}(r\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{h_r}.

(132)

Proof.

Taking
\varphi(t)=e^{-Kt}
in the previous computation, we find that

k_{\zeta,r}(W,W^{\prime})=k_{\zeta,r}^{(1)}(W,W^{\prime})+k_{\zeta,r}^{(2)}(W,W^{\prime}),

where

\displaystyle k_{\zeta,r}^{(1)}(W,W^{\prime})

\displaystyle=q\mathbb{E}(P_{W}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})^{\top}\varPsi e^{-KM}W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}P_{W^{\prime}}^{\perp}W_{*}e^{-KM^{\prime}}(\varPsi^{\prime})^{\top}(P_{W^{\prime}}\nabla\bar{H}_{r})

\displaystyle k_{\zeta,r}^{(2)}(W,W^{\prime})

\displaystyle=q\mathbb{E}\Tr W(P_{W}\nabla^{2}\bar{H}_{r}){W^{\top}W_{*}e^{-KM}W_{*}^{\top}P_{W}^{\perp}}W^{\prime}(P_{W^{\prime}}\nabla^{2}\bar{H}_{r})(W^{\prime})^{\top}W_{*}e^{-KM^{\prime}}W_{*}^{\top}P_{W^{\prime}}^{\perp},

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=\left[(\Tr\exp(-K M))(\Tr\exp(-K M'))\right]^{-1}

For the case
W=W^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle k_{\zeta,r}(W,W^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{2r}{p}(\Tr\exp(-K M))^{-2}\Tr\exp(-2K M)M(I-M)

\displaystyle=\frac{2r}{p}(\Tr\exp(-K \Sigma^2))^{-2}\Tr\exp(-2K \Sigma^2)\Sigma^{2}(I-\Sigma^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2r}{p}(\Tr\exp(-K \Sigma^2))^{-2}\Tr\exp(-2K \Sigma^2)

\displaystyle\leq 2r.

For the derivative, we can use the same argument as in the previous proof.
The only difference is that we have to consider additionally the matrix derivative of

f(M)=(\Tr e^{-KM})^{-1}e^{-KM}
.
We have

\derivative{t}f(M)=(\Tr e^{-KM})^{-1}\derivative{t}e^{-KM}-(\Tr e^{-KM})^{-2}(\derivative{t}\Tr e^{-KM})e^{-KM}=I+II.

The second term is easy to bound.
Using the property of matrix derivative and trace, we have

\derivative{t}\Tr e^{-KM}=-K\Tr e^{-KM}\dot{M},

so

\displaystyle\norm{(\Tr e^{-KM})^{-2} (\dv{t} \Tr e^{-KM}) e^{-KM}}

\displaystyle=K(\Tr e^{-KM})^{-2}\absolutevalue{\Tr e^{-KM} \dot{M}}\norm{e^{-KM}}

\displaystyle\leq K(\Tr e^{-KM})^{-2}\Tr e^{-KM}\norm{\dot{M}}\Tr e^{-KM}

\displaystyle=K\norm{\dot{M}},

where we use the fact that
\absolutevalue{\Tr A B}\leq\norm{AB}_{1}\leq\Tr A\norm{B}
for positive semi-definite matrices
A
,
where
\norm{\cdot}_{1}
is the trace norm.
For the first term, we use the matrix derivative formula that

\derivative{t}\phi(M)=Q(R\odot(Q^{\top}\dot{M}Q))Q^{\top},

where
M=Q\Lambda Q^{\top}
is the spectral decomposition of
M
,

R_{ij}=\begin{cases}\frac{\phi(\lambda_{i})-\phi(\lambda_{j})}{\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}}&\text{if }\lambda_{i}\neq\lambda_{j}\\
\phi^{\prime}(\lambda_{i})&\text{if }\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}\end{cases}

In our case, we note that
\phi(t)=e^{-Kt}
,
\phi^{\prime}(t)=-Ke^{-Kt}
, so using the mean value theorem, we have

R_{ij}=\phi^{\prime}(\xi_{ij})=-Ke^{-K\xi_{ij}},\qquad\absolutevalue{R_{ij}}\leq Ke^{-K\lambda_{\min}},

where
\xi_{ij}
is between
\lambda_{i}
and
\lambda_{j}
and
\lambda_{\min}=\min_{i}\lambda_{i}
.
Consequently,

\norm{\dv{t} e^{-KM}}_{2}=\norm{Q (R\odot(Q^\T\dot{M} Q)) Q^\T}_{2}=\norm{R\odot(Q^\T\dot{M} Q)}_{2}\leq Ke^{-K\lambda_{\min}}\norm{Q^\T\dot{M} Q}_{2}\leq Ke^{-K\lambda_{\min}}\norm{\dot{M}}_{2},

and hence

\norm{I}\leq\norm{I}_{2}\leq K\frac{e^{-K\lambda_{\min}}}{\Tr e^{-KM}}\norm{\dot{M}}_{2}\leq K\norm{\dot{M}}_{2}.

In summary, we have

\norm{\dv{t} f(M)}\leq 2K\norm{\dot{M}}_{2}.

Consequently, we can conclude that the derivative of
k_{\zeta,r}(W,W^{\prime})
is bounded by a polynomial in
p,r
.
Applying Lemma
F.3
together with a union bound, we obtain the desired bound.

∎

E.4.3
Training dynamics

Proposition E.23
.

Under Assumption
5
, assume
n\gtrsim d^{2m_{0}+1+s}
for some
s>0
.
Let (
130
) and (
132
) hold.
Let
\delta>0
be fixed.
Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, when
n,d
is large enough,
we have

\min_{1\leq i\leq p}\sigma_{i}^{2}(t)\geq 1/2,\quad\forall t\in[T^{\mathrm{app}},T^{\mathrm{fin}}],

(133)

where
T^{\mathrm{app}}\lesssim\log d+d^{m_{0}-1}
and
T^{\mathrm{fin}}
can be taken as any fixed polynomial in
d
,
and the constant in the
\lesssim
notation can depend on
\delta
.

Proof.

The proof follows similar strategies as in Proposition
D.10
and Proposition
E.17
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
h_{r}\geq 0
for all
r
.

First, according to Proposition
E.6
, there is some
c=c(\delta)
such that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\min_{1\leq i\leq p}\sigma_{i}^{2}\geq cd^{-1}.

Let us take
\omega
and
\phi_{1}
as in (
95
).
According to Proposition
F.7
, by taking
K\asymp Cd\log p
, we have

\omega(0)\geq cd^{-1}-\frac{1}{K}\log p\geq cd^{-1},

Moreover, we claim that we will have
\omega(t)\geq\rho_{0}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}cd^{-1}
for the range of
t
we are interested in.
We will prove this claim later.

Recall the dynamics in Proposition
E.19
.
Let us take
{r_{0}}=m_{0}/2
.
Noticing that when
\omega(t)\geq\rho_{0}
, we have

\phi_{{r_{0}}}(t)\geq\omega(t)^{{r_{0}}}\gtrsim d^{-{r_{0}}}\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}},

since we have
n\gtrsim d^{2m_{0}+1+s}
.
Consequently, we have

\dot{h}_{{r_{0}}}=\mu_{2{r_{0}}}(\phi_{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}-h_{{r_{0}}}+e_{{r_{0}}})\geq\mu_{2{r_{0}}}(\phi_{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}/2-h_{{r_{0}}}),

(134)

and thus

T_{0}^{g}\coloneqq\inf{\left\{t\geq 0:g_{{r_{0}}}(t)\geq\frac{1}{4}\rho_{0}^{{r_{0}}}g_{{r_{0}}}^{*}\right\}}\lesssim 1.

Now let us prove the claim for
t\leq T_{0}^{g}
.
We recall the dynamics of
\omega
in Proposition
E.19
:

\dot{\omega}=\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{i}}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})A_{i}+\zeta,

where
A_{i}=4\sum_{r\geq 1}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}
.
Similar to Proposition
D.8
, we can show that

h_{r}(t)\geq-C\min(1,\mu_{2r}t)\absolutevalue{e_r}\geq-C\min(1,\mu_{2r}t)\sqrt{\frac{d\log r}{n}}.

Also, similar to (
91
), we have

\sum_{r\geq 1}(r\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{h_r}\lesssim(1+\log^{+}t)^{2},

(135)

so the error term (
132
) is further bounded by

\absolutevalue{\zeta(W,g)}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{pd}{n}}\sqrt{\log(pK)}\sum_{r\geq 1}(r\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\absolutevalue{h_r}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{d\log d}{n}}(1+\log^{+}t)^{2}

(136)

Therefore,

\displaystyle A_{i}

\displaystyle\gtrsim-\sum_{r\geq 1}h^{*}_{r}\min(1,\mu_{2r}t)\sqrt{\frac{d\log r}{n}}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim-\sqrt{d/n}\sum_{r\geq 1}h^{*}_{r}r(\log r)^{\frac{1}{2}}\min(1,\mu_{2r}t)

\displaystyle\gtrsim-\sqrt{d/n}(1+\log^{+}t)^{2}

(137)

where we use
\mu_{r}\lesssim e^{-\gamma r}
and Proposition
F.6
in the last step.
Plugging these two bound into the dynamics of
\dot{\omega}
, we find that

\displaystyle\dot{\omega}

\displaystyle\gtrsim-\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{i}}\sqrt{d/n}(1+\log^{+}t)^{2}-\sqrt{\frac{d\log d}{n}}(1+(\log^{+}t)^{2}))

\displaystyle\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{d\log d}{n}}(1+\log^{+}t)^{2}

Consequently, since
T_{0}^{g}\lesssim 1
and
n\gtrsim d^{2m_{0}+1+s}
, we have

\omega(T_{0}^{g})\geq\omega(0)-\sqrt{\frac{d\log d}{n}}T_{0}^{g}\geq cd^{-1}-\sqrt{\frac{d\log d}{n}}\geq\rho_{0},

which proves the claim until
T_{0}^{g}
.

After that, we have

\displaystyle h_{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}={r_{0}}}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}

\displaystyle\geq\rho_{0}^{{r_{0}}}(h_{{r_{0}}}^{*})^{2}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}={r_{0}}}r_{i}\nu_{{r_{0}}}^{2}\rho_{0}^{{r_{0}}}

\displaystyle=c(h_{{r_{0}}}^{*})^{2}\rho_{0}^{2{r_{0}}}\gtrsim d^{-2{r_{0}}},

so since
n\gtrsim d^{2m_{0}+1+s}
, we have

\displaystyle A_{i}

\displaystyle=4h_{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}={r_{0}}}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}+4\sum_{r\neq{r_{0}}}h_{r}h^{*}_{r}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}=r}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}

\displaystyle\geq 4h_{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}={r_{0}}}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}-\sqrt{d/n}(1+\log^{+}t)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq 2h_{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}={r_{0}}}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}},

(138)

provided that
t
is at most polynomially large in
d
and
n
.
Thus,

\dot{\omega}\geq\frac{2}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{j}^{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-K\sigma_{i}}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})h_{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}={r_{0}}}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}\geq 0.

(139)

Consequently, with (
134
) and (
139
),
we can apply the same argument as in Proposition
E.17
.
Defining similar times
T_{k}^{g}
and
T_{k}^{\rho}
, we have
T^{\mathrm{app}}=T_{L}^{\rho}\lesssim\log d+d^{m_{0}-1}
,
where
L=1+\lceil\log_{2}\rho_{0}^{-1}\rceil
.
∎

Proposition E.24
.

Under Assumption
5
, assume
n\gtrsim d^{2m_{0}+1+s}
for some
s>0
.
Let (
130
), (
131
) hold with
\nu=1/n
.
Suppose (
133
) holds.
Then, there is some
t_{1}\leq T^{\mathrm{app}}+C
such that

1-\phi_{1}(t_{1}+s)\lesssim\exp(- c s),

provided that

1-\phi_{1}(t_{1}+s)\gtrsim\frac{pd\cdot\mathrm{polylog}(n,d,p)}{n}.

Proof.

First, using the dynamics (
134
), we find that we will have
h_{{r_{0}}}\geq ch^{*}_{{r_{0}}}
after
t_{1}=T^{\mathrm{app}}+C
.
Then, using (
138
) and (
133
), we have

A_{i}\gtrsim h_{{r_{0}}}h_{{r_{0}}}^{*}\sum_{\absolutevalue{\rr}={r_{0}}}r_{i}\nu_{{\bm{r}}}^{2}\sigma^{2{\bm{r}}}\gtrsim 1.

Consequently, the dynamics of
\phi_{1}
in Proposition
E.19
gives

\dot{\phi}_{1}\geq c\sum_{i=1}^{p}(1-\sigma_{i}^{2})-\absolutevalue{\xi}=c(1-\phi_{1})-\absolutevalue{\xi}.

On the other hand, combining (
131
) with (
135
) and that the time is at most polynomially large in
d
,
we have

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\xi}\lesssim(1-\phi_{1})^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{dp\mathrm{polylog}(n,d)}{n}}.

Therefore, as long as
1-\phi_{1}\gtrsim\frac{dp\mathrm{polylog}(n,d,p)}{n}
, we have
\absolutevalue{\xi}\leq c(1-\phi_{1})/2
, so

\dot{\phi}_{1}\geq c(1-\phi_{1})/2
,
yielding the desired result.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

The initial feature error measure is an easy consequence of Proposition
E.6
.
For the decay of the feature error measure, se apply Proposition
E.23
and Proposition
E.24
with error bounds Proposition
E.20
, Proposition
E.21
and Proposition
E.22
.
For the final feature error measure, we can use Proposition
E.14
and the fact that

1-\phi_{1}\leq\nu
implies
1-\min_{j}\sigma_{j}^{2}\leq p\nu
.
∎

Appendix F
Auxiliary results

F.1
Random process

Definition F.1
.

Let
X(\cdot)
be a random process on a metric space
(T,d)
.
We say that
X(\cdot)
is
\sigma^{2}
-sub-Gaussian if

\displaystyle\norm{X(t) - X(s)}_{\psi_{2}}^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}d(s,t)^{2},\quad\text{for all $s,t\in T$}.

Theorem F.2

(Dudley’s entropy integral)
.

Let
X
be a
\sigma^{2}
-sub-Gaussian random process on a metric space
(T,d)
and
\mathbb{E}X(t)=0
.
Let
\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,T,d)
be the covering number of
T
with respect to
d
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}X(t)\leq C\sigma\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,T,d)}\differential\varepsilon.

(140)

Also, for any
u\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-2\exp(-u^2)
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s,t\in T}\absolutevalue{X(s) - X(t)}\leq C\sigma\left(\int_{0}^{\mathrm{diam}(T,d)}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,T,d)}\differential\varepsilon+u\cdot\mathrm{diam}(T,d)\right).

(141)

Let us now consider a mean-zero Gaussian process
X(t),t\in T
for
T\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
The covariance function of
X(\cdot)
is given by
k(s,t)=\mathbb{E}X(s)X(t)
.
We further assume that
k(t,t)=1
for all
t\in T
.
The induced metric is given by

d(s,t)=\sqrt{2(1-k(s,t))}.

We have the following result on the covering number of
T
with respect to
d
.

Lemma F.3
.

Let
X(t),t\in T\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}
be a mean-zero Gaussian process with covariance function
k(s,t)
.
Suppose that
k(t,t)\leq 1
for all
t\in T
and
k
is Hölder continuous with exponent
\alpha
on the diagonal:

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{k(t,t) - k(s,t)}\leq L\norm{t-s}^{\alpha}.

Let
R=\mathrm{diam}(T,\norm{\cdot})
.
Then,

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}X(t)\leq C\alpha^{-1/2}(\sqrt{\log R+\log L+C})\sqrt{p}.

Also, for any
u\geq 0
, with probability at least
1-4\exp(-u^2)
, we have

\sup_{t\in T}\absolutevalue{X(t)}\leq C\alpha^{-1/2}(\sqrt{\log R+\log L+C})\sqrt{p}+Cu.

Proof.

Let us denote by
d(s,t)
the induced metric.
As
d(s,t)\leq 1
, we have
\mathrm{diam}(T,d)\leq\sqrt{2}
.
We denote by
R=\mathrm{diam}(T,\norm{\cdot})
.
Then, we find that

d(s,t)=\sqrt{2(1-k(s,t))}=\sqrt{2(k(t,t)-k(s,t))}\leq\sqrt{2L\left(\norm{t - s}^{\alpha}\right)}=\sqrt{2L}\norm{t - s}^{\alpha/2}.

This shows that

\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,T,d)\leq\mathcal{N}((\varepsilon/\sqrt{2L})^{2/\alpha},T,\norm{\cdot}).

Now, the standard result on the covering number of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
gives

\mathcal{N}(\delta,T,\norm{\cdot})\leq(CR/\delta)^{p}
,
so

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}((\varepsilon/\sqrt{2L})^{2/\alpha},T,\norm{\cdot})\leq(CR/(\varepsilon/\sqrt{2L})^{2/\alpha})^{p}=\left(CR(2L)^{1/\alpha}\right)^{p}\varepsilon^{-2p/\alpha}.

Plugging this into Dudley’s entropy integral, we find that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,T,d)}\differential\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{\log\left(CRL^{1/\alpha}\right)^{p}(\varepsilon/\sqrt{2})^{-2p/\alpha}}\differential\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{p(\log(CR)+\alpha^{-1}\log(2L))+\frac{2p}{\alpha}\log(\sqrt{2}/\ep)}\differential\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}}\left[\sqrt{p(\log(CR)+\alpha^{-1}\log(2L))}+\sqrt{\frac{2p}{\alpha}\log(\sqrt{2}/\ep)}\right]\differential\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\sqrt{p(\log(CR)+\alpha^{-1}\log(2L))}+\sqrt{2p/\alpha}C

\displaystyle\leq C\alpha^{-1/2}(\sqrt{\log R+\log L+C})\sqrt{p}

∎

F.2
Sequence model

Lemma F.4
.

Consider the sequence model
z_{j}=f^{*}_{j}+\varepsilon_{j},~j\in N
induced by an orthogonal basis.
For a function
f
and its coefficients
f_{j}
under the basis, define the population loss and the empirical loss as

\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in N}(f^{*}_{j}-f_{j})^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\norm{f^* - f}_{L^{2}}^{2}
and
\hat{\mathcal{L}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j\in N}(z_{j}-f_{j})^{2}
.
Then, we have

-\nabla\hat{\mathcal{L}}=-\nabla\mathcal{L}+\sum_{j\in N}\varepsilon_{j}\nabla f_{j}.

(142)

Proof.

It is direct from the following computation:

-\nabla\hat{\mathcal{L}}=\sum_{j\in N}(z_{j}-f_{j})\nabla f_{j}=\sum_{j\in N}(f^{*}_{j}-f_{j})\nabla f_{j}+\sum_{j\in N}\varepsilon_{j}\nabla f_{j}=-\nabla\mathcal{L}+\sum_{j}\varepsilon_{j}\nabla f_{j}.

∎

F.3
Series

Proposition F.5
.

Fix
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\lambda_{r}\asymp r^{-\gamma}
for
\gamma>\max(0,\alpha+1)
.
Then, for any fixed
s>0
, we have

\sum_{r\geq 0}r^{\alpha}(\log r)^{q}\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)\lesssim 1+t^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\alpha+1+s)},

(143)

where the implicit constant depends on
\alpha,q,s,\gamma
.

Proof.

Let
L=\inf{\left\{r\geq 0:\lambda_{r}t\leq 1\right\}}\asymp t^{1/\gamma}
.
Then, we have

I=\sum_{r\geq 0}r^{\alpha}(\log r)^{q}\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)=\sum_{r\leq L}r^{\alpha}(\log r)^{q}+\sum_{r>L}r^{\alpha}(\log r)^{q}\lambda_{r}t=I_{1}+I_{2}.

For
I_{1}
, we have

I_{1}=\sum_{r\leq L}r^{\alpha}(\log r)^{q}\lesssim\begin{cases}1,&\alpha<-1,\\
L^{\alpha+1+s}\lesssim t^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\alpha+1+s)},&\alpha\geq-1.\end{cases}

For
I_{2}
, we have

I_{2}=\sum_{r>L}r^{\alpha}(\log r)^{q}\lambda_{r}t\lesssim t\sum_{r>L}r^{\alpha}(\log r)^{q}r^{-\gamma}=t\sum_{r>L}r^{-\gamma+\alpha}(\log r)^{q}\lesssim tL^{-\gamma+\alpha+1+s}\lesssim t^{\frac{1}{\gamma}(\alpha+1+s)}.

The result follows from the above two inequalities.
∎

Proposition F.6
.

Fix
p,q\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\lambda_{r}\lesssim e^{-\gamma r}
for
\gamma>0
.
Then, for any fixed
s>0
, we have

\sum_{r\geq 0}r^{p}(\log r)^{q}\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)\lesssim 1+(\log^{+}t)^{p+1+s}

(144)

where the implicit constant depends on
p,q,s,\gamma
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
p,q\geq 0
and
t\geq 1
.
Let us define
L=\inf{\left\{r\geq 0:\lambda_{r}t\leq 1\right\}}\lesssim\gamma^{-1}\log t
.
Then, we have

I=\sum_{r\geq 0}r^{p}(\log r)^{q}\min(1,\lambda_{r}t)=\sum_{r\leq L}r^{p}(\log r)^{q}+\sum_{r>L}r^{p}(\log r)^{q}\lambda_{r}t=I_{1}+I_{2}.

For
I_{1}
, we have

I_{1}=\sum_{r\leq L}r^{p}(\log r)^{q}\lesssim\begin{cases}1,&p<-1,\\
L^{p+1+s},&p\geq-1.\end{cases}

For
I_{2}
, we have

I_{2}=\sum_{r>L}r^{p}(\log r)^{q}\lambda_{r}t\lesssim t\sum_{r>L}r^{p}(\log r)^{q}e^{-\gamma r}\lesssim tL^{p+s}\exp(-\gamma L)\lesssim L^{p+s}.

Combining the above two inequalities with
L\lesssim\log t
, we prove the result.
∎

F.4
Some Elementary Functions

Proposition F.7
.

Let
x_{i}\in[0,1]
,
i=1,\dots,p
.
Let
K>0
.
Define the function

\omega(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})=-\frac{1}{K}\log\left(\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-Kx_{i}}\right).

Then,

\min(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})\geq\omega(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})\geq\min(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})-\frac{1}{K}\log p.

Moreover,

\partialderivative{x_i}\omega=\frac{e^{-Kx_{i}}}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-Kx_{j}}}.

Proposition F.8
.

The following inequality holds for any
x_{i}\in[0,1]
,
i=1,\dots,p
and
K>0
:

\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}e^{-Kx_{j}}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}e^{-Kx_{i}}(1-x_{i})\geq 1-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}x_{i}.
